Solana Price Struggles Below $90: Is a Bullish Reversal Taking Shape?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Solana’s price decline below $90 has put its recovery prospects at risk, but a potential bullish signal is emerging beneath the ongoing sell-off. While SOL continues to record lower lows, its RSI is moving in the opposite direction, hinting that bearish momentum may be losing strength.

At the same time, 12 consecutive sessions without net inflows into spot Solana ETFs highlight weakening institutional demand. This creates a critical battle between fading selling pressure and a lack of fresh capital. Could this RSI divergence signal Solana’s next rebound, or is another leg lower still ahead?

Solana is trading near $89.90 after breaking below an important support area. The decline follows a broader correction from its October highs, with the token continuing to form lower highs and lower lows.

The latest breakdown also reflects weakening buying interest. SOL has struggled to regain its short-term moving averages, while the broader price structure continues to favour sellers. The loss of support has further weakened the recovery attempt, leaving the token vulnerable to continued volatility.

Institutional flows are adding another layer of pressure to Solana’s recovery prospects. According to the latest market update, spot Solana ETFs have recorded no net inflows for 12 consecutive trading sessions.

JUST IN: 🇺🇸 US spot crypto ETF outflows on Nov 6.• 📉 Bitcoin ($BTC): -$412M• 📉 Ethereum ($ETH): -$93M• 📉 Solana ($SOL): -$25M pic.twitter.com/Muj58z2e4E

The reported data shows that Bitwise and other ETF clients sold approximately $25.08 million worth of SOL in the latest session. The extended absence of positive flows suggests that institutional demand has weakened during the ongoing correction.

ETF activity remains an important indicator of investor appetite, particularly as institutional products provide another route for traditional investors to gain exposure to Solana. A return to consistent inflows could help improve market confidence, while continued outflows may make a sustained recovery harder to establish.

Despite the continued decline, Solana’s technical indicators are showing a potentially encouraging development. Its price is making lower lows, while the RSI is moving higher, creating a bullish divergence.

$SOL is going down, but RSI is trending upThis is what happens when spot sellers are exhausted And every time, seller exhaustion brings a big pump pic.twitter.com/KkCRLxJdwf

This pattern suggests that selling momentum may be weakening even though the price remains under pressure. Such divergences can emerge near the later stages of a correction, when sellers begin losing strength and buyers gradually return.

However, RSI divergence does not guarantee an immediate reversal. SOL still needs stronger buying participation and a meaningful improvement in its price structure to confirm that the downtrend is losing control.

If the divergence develops into a broader recovery signal, it could create an opportunity for Solana to stabilise after its recent losses.

Solana’s near-term outlook remains cautious as weak ETF flows and a deteriorating price structure continue to weigh on the token. The lack of institutional demand also raises questions about how quickly buyers could return following the recent breakdown.

However, the improving RSI presents a potential counter-signal. If selling pressure continues to fade and spot demand recovers, SOL could attempt to reverse its recent losses.

The next phase will depend on whether this technical divergence attracts fresh buying interest or simply provides a temporary pause in the broader decline. For now, Solana’s recovery remains uncertain, but weakening selling momentum offers a reason to watch for a possible change in trend.

PLL's Lucy Hale and Ian Harding Reveal If They Will Work Together Again

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

12 Dates of Christmas allowed Lucy Hale and Ian Harding to play love interests again after Pretty Little Liars — but do they have plans to reunite again in the future?

“From your lips to God’s ears. How fun would that be?” Hale, 37, said during an exclusive interview with Us, to which Harding, 40, replied, “What if we just went through every genre?”

The pair suggested a horror movie next where they are a “newly married couple” but one of them “is a ghost.” For Hale, the project didn’t matter as long as she got to share the screen again with Harding.

“Ian actually is the best. I love working with you. I love you as a person,” she told him. “And it was just nice to have all that built in history and something new to do.”

Harding felt the same way, adding, “It was so fantastic. We were kind of remarking on this earlier about how we just stepped back into it. There’s the novelty of a new movie, a new set that you’re on and your very first day. Maybe there’s a little bit of jitters but I felt that way for about seven seconds and then it just was good to be back and getting to play with a dear friend.”

Hale and Harding captured our hearts when they played Aria and Ezra, respectively, in Pretty Little Liars. After the show came to an end in 2017, Hale and Harding weren’t in a rush to find their next collaboration.

“This was our first attempt and our last. No, I’m kidding,” she quipped. “This was our first attempt. Of course we’ve kept in touch here and there. But we live across the country from each other.”

Hale continued: “I just thought he was perfect for this and I was hopeful that he would say yes.”

Harding was thrilled at the prospect of sharing the screen with Hale again, telling Us, “I obviously enjoyed working with Lucy many years ago and I also know the love that people have for that show and perhaps even our dynamic. So I didn’t want to waste it on something ridiculous and so I loved this idea because it kind of plays on certain tropes. I just wanted to really play with her so this gave us the vehicle to do it.”

12 Dates of Christmas follows a woman who has prioritized her work life over her personal life and is persuaded to go on 12 dates in 12 weeks just in time for Christmas. One of the disastrous dates was played by Carter Jenkins, which served as a throwback for Hale after she went out with the actor in real life.

“I’ve known since I was 15. He was actually my first boyfriend. That was my first boyfriend and we’ve been friends ever since,” Hale told Us. “He’s the best. I can’t remember where he took me on a date. I think the date was a movie he was in. And I think we got sushi with his mom after.”

While Hale’s character went on 12 disastrous dates, the actress didn’t pull much inspiration from her life, telling Us, “I’ve been fairly lucky. I never did the dating thing in my 20s. I was always in monogamous relationships. But I did do a fair share of dating a couple of years ago. But I must be very lucky because I don’t have any disastrous dates. Compared to [my character] Christina in [the] movie, nothing too crazy.”

12 Dates of Christmas is streaming now on Hulu.

Risk of Iran war escalation remains high despite Democratic sweep in U.S. midterm elections

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The U.S. and Iran are careening toward renewed war despite growing hopes this week that the Democrats' decisive midterm election victory will bring the conflict to an end, analysts say.

Crude oil prices fell more than 16% over the last five trading days after Vice President JD Vance publicly blamed the Republicans' stinging loss on the war. Vance's remarks raised questions about whether there is still support within the administration to continue the conflict with Iran.

The Democrats plan to use their comfortable majorities in both chambers of Congress to pass a war powers resolution that demands President Donald Trump end the war. But tensions in the Middle East have not eased since the election, and the Democrats aren't sworn in until January, the analysts say.

"Throughout the week, we were struck by the divergence between deepening escalation concerns in the Gulf and the headline driven off-ramp optimism of market participants thousands of miles away," Helima Croft, head of global commodity strategy at RBC Capital Markets, told investors in a note Friday.

Several oil tankers have come under attack near Oman since the election, killing one seafarer, according to maritime security agencies. Iran's Houthi allies in Yemen continue to fire missiles at neighboring Saudi Arabia.

Meanwhile, the U.S. military buildup continues, with a third carrier strike group en route to the region. The consulting group Rapidan Energy sees an 80% chance that fighting will resume as diplomacy stalls.

President Donald Trump told reporters Thursday that he will make a decision soon on whether to attack Iran again.

"I would rather not destroy the rest of the country if possible but maybe I'll have to," Trump said in the Oval Office. "You'll know soon enough."

"We're locked and loaded," the president said. "If we have to do the rest of the Iran thing, we're very much locked in."

Crude oil prices will vault above $100 a barrel again in response to renewed fighting, said Bob McNally, president of Rapidan and a former energy advisor to President George W. Bush. Brent crude, the international benchmark, was trading above $97 on Friday while U.S. crude was around $90.

"President Donald Trump is determined as a legacy item, almost at all costs, to prevent Iran from getting a nuclear weapon, having them give up their nuclear capabilities," McNally told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Friday.

"The Iranian regime, on the other hand, is not willing to do it," the analyst said. The escalation dynamic remains in place so long as that core difference between the U.S. and Iran persists, he said.

The risk of war is still alive, especially since Trump doesn't face anymore future elections, said Amos Hochstein, a former energy advisor to President Joe Biden. Gasoline, diesel and jet fuel prices would all surge as a consequence, Hochstein told CNBC's "Power Lunch" Friday.

Tehran has been taunting the Trump administration since the election. The social media account of Iran's Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei said the "process of the U.S.'s collapse has accelerated" after the midterm results.

And Congress is unlikely to pass a war powers resolution until Democrats take control in January. Trump can veto such a resolution in any case.

The main leverage Congress will have is the power of the purse, Croft said. Government funding expires on Dec. 11 and negotiations for another continuing resolution could drag into 2027, she said. This would give Democrats more leverage when they take power in January, the analyst said.

"Importantly, deferring funding decisions would not immediately constrain Trump's ability to conduct military operations, but could significantly reshape the administration's longer-term funding outlook," Croft told investors.

Trump discussed diplomatic efforts to de-escalate tensions in the region with the emir of Qatar during a Friday phone call, according to Qatar's state news agency.

An unnamed senior Iranian official told Reuters on Thursday that Tehran sent a nuclear proposal to the U.S. through Qatar. Tehran will only discuss the proposal after the U.S. meets its demands, the Iranian official told Reuters.

Trump seemed to dismiss the diplomatic efforts Thursday.

"Iran will never have a nuclear weapon," the president said. "They're not doing anything without our approval. It doesn't make any difference what they're looking at," he said of the diplomatic efforts.

I Added 10 More Miles a Week to Marathon Training—and Seriously Felt the Difference at Mile 20

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

How a modest mileage bump helped one marathoner finish stronger, feel better, and build confidence.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

4:09:48. That was my finish time at this year’s New York City Marathon. It was also—to the second—my finish time in the same race last year.

Putting aside the wildness of that (but what does it mean?) I learned a few lessons as I cruised through the five boroughs for the seventh time.

Specifically, although I ran the exact same time, my splits looked quite different. I ran the latter miles faster and they felt easier this year. And that comes down to one key training adjustment I made: adding more overall mileage to my week.

Here’s how and why I maximized my volume during this training cycle, and how it paid off in my strongest finish yet (even if not a PR).

I ran the London Marathon in April, and both New York and Sydney last year, using an artificial intelligence training app. I programmed in that I wanted to run three days a week so I could continue to strength train two to three days a week as well. Plus, I walk quite a bit as someone who lives in New York City (around four to five miles a day on top of any running).

But then I reported on a series of articles about durability for Runner’s World and I started thinking more about total weekly mileage and how much I had (or had not) been doing. Turns out, I peaked around 30 to 35 miles for all three of those marathons. It made me realize there was room for growth.

When I got the opportunity to run New York again this year, I figured out how high I could go mileage-wise without upping my risk for injury.

I started by talking to Andrew Kastor, head coach of the Asics Mammoth Track Club. Before our call, I sent him the training plan I planned to follow for this upcoming training cycle: the Runner’s World Break Four Hours plan. (I also intended to continue strength training twice a week.)

The peak week mileage for that plan: 48 miles, with at least four days a week of running. This may not seem like a ton to people who swear by super high mileage, but it is a steep increase relative to my previous builds. “Between 32 and 50 is night and day,” Kastor says. “You’re going to notice a big difference in your staying power after mile 20.”

Importantly, I built a pretty solid base before starting the training plan 16 weeks out from race day and felt ready to jump into week one when the time came. This plan also leverages mileage ranges, rather than firm numbers, allowing me to further customize it.

Kastor likes the approach of mileage ranges and uses it with many of his athletes. “It kind of takes a little bit of the edge off or the pressure off,” he says, because you can choose the lower end of the range if you’re feeling fatigued.

He also advised me to add in the optional up-to-three-mile run in my plan every other week (where you see 0 to 3 miles). “It’ll be worth it the last mile of the race or the last two miles of the race [when] you’re able to hold that 9:07 pace or that nine flat pace,” he says.

Kastor encouraged me to pay attention to how I was feeling as the training stacked up, and to pull back if I noticed symptoms of overtraining, like an elevated resting heart rate, reduced heart rate variability, or subjective measures like excessive fatigue or feeling flat in workouts. “If [the] legs are not bouncing back week after week, then that’s a sign of overtraining and that you need to pull back and not just pull back with mileage, but take two days off to let your legs recover and heal up,” he says.

I actually felt great throughout my training, which I also attribute to switching up my strength training a bit under the guidance of my physical therapist (I focused more on heavier weight and much less volume, while adding isometrics for tendon health.) Mentally, I also started looking more forward to my running days versus my off days, which is likely due to the fact that I was running more regularly.

In addition to watching for signs of overtraining, Kastor also emphasized the importance of recovery and nutrition when increasing mileage. “In order to do more mileage, you have to sleep well and eat well,” he says.

I focused specifically on eating more carbs than I ever have in training before. (I recently realized I was so focused on protein that I was coming up short on carbs.) As for sleep, I’m always pretty protective of that. So I just kept doing what I was doing: aiming for eight to nine hours a night.

Per Kastor’s suggestion, I also committed to a sports massage every other week during my training, and used the Therabody JetBoots PRO Plus immediately after long runs and occasionally in the evenings on other days. (I feel like they relax my whole nervous system before bed.)

Modern running shoes can also help you handle more time on your feet, Kastor explains. “Generally, because people can handle a little bit more volume with the cushion of the shoes, they’re not breaking down as much,” he says.

I trained in a higher cushioned shoe—the Asics Gel-Nimbus 28—which is known for its premium cushioned, long-lasting ride, according to Runner’s World reviewers. It provided me with a high-energy return and support for the extra miles.

When I spoke to experts in durability about how to increase it for late-race performance, they told me that weekly mileage plays a major role. Research, too, backs up the importance of volume, stating long runs of at least 90 minutes and higher training volumes overall improve durability. And other research suggests more volume (especially easy miles) can lead to faster marathon finish times.

“We start where the athlete is with their fitness and then we progress upward because volume is king,” Kastor says. “More is better as long as you stay healthy.”

He adds that there’s something about the repetition of running frequently that seems to help, citing his wife, marathon Olympic bronze medalist Deena Kastor, as an example. At one point, she was running upward of 140 miles, hitting 13 runs a week, he says. “She would wake up from a nap and be really sleepy, rubbing her eyes, lace up her shoes, and go for a run,” he says. “I think what’s overlooked generally is the role of the nervous system and [how it] plays into the efficiency of running mechanics. If you [run] over and over and over and over again, then you get really, really good at it.”

So, you know I did not PR after adding more mileage to my training plan. But! Take a look at the times for the final four miles in 2025 versus 2026:

I was clearly significantly stronger during the latter miles this year than I was last year, and it was amazing to see and feel that difference. In 2025, I’m honestly surprised I was able to hold the paces I did—I remember feeling pretty wiped, which is why I held back some this time. This year, I felt like I was cruising down 59th Street on the way to mile 25, and even later in the day, I felt good enough to walk almost a mile to dinner and back home.

I felt so strong at the end of this year’s marathon that I know the “mistake” I made was to not trust my training earlier in the race. I was so afraid that I would run out of steam if I held the 8:47 pace I hit at miles 4 and 5 that I pulled back.

So for me, the race was much more of a mental one than a physical one. Now I just have to trust that next time, I can hold faster paces earlier on and still finish strong.

At the end of the day, I realize that I was so focused on my physical training plan and even nutrition that I didn’t think about the psychology of it all. Because I’d run all my long runs at a much easier pace (with the exception of a handful that called for some tempo miles in the middle), I kind of freaked out at the idea of running 26 miles at a 9:00 pace. Now, I truly believe I can. And more mileage is definitely a big part of why.

Anthropic IPO meets AI backlash after midterms show stinging rebuke of data centers

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Americans were unequivocal at the ballot box this week as they expressed disdain for tech billionaires and mistrust of artificial intelligence. With that backdrop, Anthropic is about to try to go public.

The AI company is gearing up for a potentially historic IPO, likely targeting mid-November for its Nasdaq debut. CNBC reported on Friday that Anthropic investors see the company aiming for a valuation of $1 trillion to $1.5 trillion, down from a target of $2 trillion that had been floated in recent months.

From Wall Street to Main Street, it's becoming a tougher environment for Anthropic. The Nasdaq on Thursday suffered its steepest drop in 19 months, and it's down to start November after October marked its worst month since March 2025. Chipmakers and neoclouds, the companies most closely tied to the AI buildout, are getting hit the hardest.

Voters rebuked AI in Tuesday's midterm elections, while sending Democrats to sweeping congressional victories across the country. Candidates who advocated for more stringent AI regulation won handily in states like Michigan, Ohio, Texas and Georgia, which will all now have Democratic governors, and local communities opposed data center-related ballot measures in 40 out of 41 cases.

More than half of voters said AI data centers were a "crucial" or "major factor" in who they supported for Congress, according to an exit poll from NBC News.

"People don't want a data center going near them if it's just going to house AI that's going to help a big tech company profit," Maura Austin, a behavioral scientist at the University of Virginia, told CNBC. "What Anthropic is doing when they're going public with such a high valuation is they're really leaning into that big tech, billionaire kind of caricature. I expect that's not going to go well with the public."

Anthropic has long tried to position itself as a safety-conscious company that takes AI's potential risks and benefits seriously. CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company alongside a handful of researchers who defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and it primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

The company's enterprise focus has helped it avoid becoming the public face of AI, leaving that distinction to OpenAI and CEO Sam Altman. But with Anthropic poised to become the first pure-play AI company to hit the public market, minting a number of new billionaires and many more millionaires in the process, a heightened level of scrutiny is coming.

"Being a public company, just that by itself pushes you in the limelight," Reena Aggarwal, a professor of finance at Georgetown University, said in an interview. "And then AI is such a hot-button issue that Anthropic will be in the limelight even more."

Matt Kennedy, a senior IPO market strategist at Renaissance Capital, said that's not necessarily a bad thing for Anthropic.

Public markets force greater transparency, he said, and the company could attract new users through its debut. And since AI has become such a mainstream conversation topic, several private companies are already household names.

"I would say that a public company does not deserve more criticisms than a comparable private company," Kennedy said. "So Anthropic should not be put in a harsher public spotlight than OpenAI just because it's public."

AI backlash accelerated over the summer after a series of high-profile safety and security incidents rattled the public. As concerns mounted, the debate over data centers intensified, while tech's hyperscalers simultaneously boosted their forecasts for capital expenditures, each reaching into the hundreds of billions of dollars.

Anthropic and OpenAI depend on state-of-the-art data centers to train models and serve customers, and they're trying to build out capacity as quickly as possible to meet demand. Anthropic said in an amended IPO prospectus this week that it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

Americans grappling with inflation and stubbornly high gas prices have grown worried that the facilities could exacerbate environmental harms, raise utility bills and contribute to job loss. For many, a proposed data center in their backyard became the physical manifestation of the widespread antipathy toward AI.

In the risk factors section of its prospectus, Anthropic said opposition to data center development has become more organized across jurisdictions, and that the company's public profile could draw particular scrutiny to projects associated with it.

Nicole Greene, an analyst at Gartner, said she expects that Anthropic's executives have long been prepared to contend with AI backlash, especially given the disruptive nature of the technology.

"There's going to be big emotions," Greene told CNBC. "With big emotions and feelings — fear and excitement — comes heightened scrutiny."

Amodei acknowledged the souring sentiment around AI in a post on X in August. He said it's the result of a "crisis of trust," and that people believe companies, the government and the tech industry are always "cooking up some new way to screw them over."

Rebuilding public confidence is going to be an uphill battle as the company begins providing quarterly financial updates.

The University of Southern California published a survey on Monday that found that 71% of respondents don't trust big tech companies to act in society's best interest "at all" around data center development.

"Once you've lost trust, it's hard to gain it back," Wändi Bruine de Bruin, a professor at the University of Southern California's Schaeffer Institute, said in an interview. "The numbers are not good."

Cosm rides premium moviegoing wave with more immersive screenings and a wider library

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The next big thing in premium large format theatrical viewing takes inspiration from the stars.

Cosm, an immersive entertainment technology company, got its start programming planetariums — its name a combination of "cosmos" and "colosseum." The company's massive, domed screens first offered guests a view of the universe, now they mimic courtside seats at sports venues around the world and drop film fans into some of Hollywood's most iconic sets.

At a time when the theatrical industry is seeing its biggest growth from the sale of premium large format, or PLF, tickets, Cosm seeks to offer a fresh take on an elevated viewing experience. And it's quickly expanding both its footprint and its content library.

Since 2024, Cosm has opened four theaters — near Los Angeles, Dallas, Atlanta and Detroit — with a Cleveland hub set to open in 2027. These experimental venues have garnered the attention of sports fans, screening live matchups in an arena-like environment. Showings often sell out, according to Cosm.

More recently, Cosm has partnered with Hollywood studios like Warner Bros., now a part of Skydance, to bring the likes of "The Matrix," "Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory," "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" and "It" to its locations. Cosm recently signed a deal with Universal, which will bring "Shrek" to its domes in December.

The agreements mark a lucrative new chapter for the privately held Cosm, expanding its audience reach and viewing opportunities beyond timed, live events.

As a privately held company, Cosm doesn't disclose its financials. Tickets cost anywhere from $18 for a matinee to around $70 for prime seats or in-demand showings. That doesn't include added fees for concessions or items from special, themed menus tied to screenings.

Sony invested $100 million in the company in June, taking a minority stake and placing Sony Pictures Entertainment Chairman and CEO Ravi Ahuja onto its board of directors.

Cosm also counts among its investors Cosm chairman Steve Winn's firm, Mirasol Capital; former Milwaukee Bucks co-owner Marc Lasry's Avenue Sports Fund; Rocket Mortgage co-founder Dan Gilbert's Rock; and David Blitzer, managing partner of the New Jersey Devils and Philadelphia 76ers, who invested through his firm Bolt Ventures.

"Cosm is really early in its growth trajectory at the moment, and we saw an opportunity to partner with the company at, what I would say is at, a very important stage in its development," Jay Levine, chief strategy and business operations officer at Sony, told CNBC. "They have about five locations with the goal of a pretty significant rollout. And the real reason why we did it was it gave us a lot of exposure to a growing distribution and what we deem an experiential platform that we think has long-term potential."

Sony has made several plays in the theatrical space in recent years. It bought Alamo Drafthouse in 2024 and entered a long-term lease to reopen and restore the iconic Cinerama Dome in Los Angeles in July of this year.

"Our experiences strategy is around tech, it's around fandom, and it's around new and innovative approaches to content distribution," Levine said. "For us, we're all about these differentiated experiences."

Cosm's 87-foot-diameter, concave screens drop viewers into the middle of the action, whether it's an NFL game or inside the world of "The Matrix." The company's forthcoming Cleveland location will have an even bigger dome, at 100 feet in diameter.

The experience is akin to that of the Sphere in Las Vegas, which has garnered headlines since opening in 2023 for hosting high-profile concerts and events.

"People absolutely love to live out what they love in fandom, just like wearing a sports jersey," Alexis Scalice, head of Cosm Studios, told CNBC. "And I love that I'm seeing that same behavior happen with cinema."

Stadium-style seating is nestled within each Cosm dome, with booths and tables set on each level. The dome's width is such that it fills much of the viewer's periphery, giving the sensation that they are part of the on-screen environment.

The Los Angeles and Dallas locations have capacity for around 350 seats each, while the newer domes in Atlanta and Detroit have up to 500 available seats. Cosm has not shared how many seats the Cleveland location will have, but with its larger screen it is expected to have a larger number of seats.

For live sports events, Cosm has specialty cameras mounted in arenas that broadcast back to the dome, creating the illusion of being on the field of play.

For theatrical features, the film is played in a large rectangle in the center of the dome and the background is transformed to extend the scene.

In Cosm's presentation of "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone," for example, during the iconic scene where thousands of Hogwarts acceptance letters flood through the fireplace and into a living room, the background is swarmed with animated envelopes.

Cosm has partnered with experiential production company Little Cinema and creative studio MakeMake to create the visuals and environments for each scene of a film released in the dome. From storyboarding to post-production, this process can take up to a year as Cosm, the participating studio and the visual effects artists craft and render each moment.

"We really work hard not to take away from the story, so the film remains the hero in the narrative," Scalice said. "Working very closely with Warner Bros. and our creative partners to make sure that we're not detracting too much from the story, but we're enhancing those moments for more elevated emotional response."

With the exception of "It," which was released in 2017, Cosm's theatrical offerings thus far have been films that were released decades ago and that typically have long-established fan bases.

That helps Cosm leverage what Scalice called "that nostalgia factor" — "parents coming in that ... grew up on this, and they want to share it with their children."

"We can reinvigorate these legendary films," she added.

Cosm has dabbled across genres — horror, fantasy, science fiction — and also demographics — family-friendly and R-rated. "Shrek," set to debut Dec. 16, will be its first foray into animation.

"The thing that's so incredible about it is everybody thought initially it's just going to be working for sports, and when you look at what they've done, a lot of the titles with 'Harry Potter,' with 'Shrek,' and some of the other ones, I mean, the fill rates are actually incredible," Sony's Levine said.

Studios and theatrical exhibitors alike are looking for innovative ways to showcase Hollywood's product to moviegoers, especially as major shifts in viewing habits have disrupted the business.

In the post-pandemic era, the cinema space has been defined by fewer screens and fewer moviegoers, but those declines have been masked by higher movie ticket prices, particularly for PLF experiences.

These elevated offerings — think Imax, Dolby Cinema, ScreenX and 4DX — have bigger screens and better sound or introduce a unique experience, such as moving seats and fog machines. They're particularly popular with Gen Z and millennials, who've yet to be deterred by rising average ticket prices.

While an average standard movie ticket costs around $13, premium tickets average more than $18 each, according to data from EntTelligence.

Prices for Cosm's events fluctuate based on showtime and venue, but on average it's "higher than I think anybody really would have thought," Sony's Levine said.

Unlike Cosm's sports offerings, which can also be viewed from outside the dome from its hall and deck viewing areas at a lower price, theatrical presentations are only available in the dome.

"They have no problem selling these tickets because people want to experience these things, like an immersive format," said Mike Polydoros, CEO at cinema marketing firm PaperAirplane Media. "That is something they can't get at home."

If you've never seen a Western before, these familiar faces could be enough to get you hooked

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Verdict: "The Magnificent Seven" is a remake of the 1960's Western of the same name. That movie was itself a remake, adapting Akira Kurosawa's 1954 classic "Seven Samurai." This updated version is not better than either of those movies; they're two of the greatest films of all time. But with its modern stars and a few excellent shootouts, it is a great choice for those looking for an entry point into the Western genre. Just make sure to watch the other two movies once you're done.

Where to watch: Stream "The Magnificent" for free on Tubi or YouTube

Going into this, I thought that "The Magnificent Seven" (2016) would be a tough movie to recommend. It's not that I expected it to be the worst film in my 52-movie-long Denzel Watchathon. I thought it would be perfectly fine, and ultimately, it did live up to those moderate expectations. The problem is, this movie is a remake of the 1960 Western classic of the same name, and that's one of the greatest Westerns ever made.

Further complicating things is that the 1960 version of "The Magnificent Seven" isn't even the best version of that story. That honor belongs to Akira Kurosawa's "Seven Samurai," a movie you likely heard of and almost certainly saw an homage to at some point. It's been remade or copied directly over a dozen times and then indirectly probably scores more. One of my favorite clones of "Seven Samurai" is the episode "Bounty Hunters" from "Star Wars: The Clone Wars."

In short, it's tough to recommend watching a movie that's an inferior copy of a copy, even if the movie itself is perfectly fine. But as I watched this latest homage to Kurosawa, I realised that there is an audience for this movie. Especially while you can still stream it for free.

If you want to watch this movie before you read on, I have good news! It's currently streaming for free on Tubi, one of the best free streaming services we've tested. You can also stream it for free on YouTube (with ads).

Malcolm has been with the Streaming team at Tom's Guide since 2023, reviewing dozens of movies each year so you don't have to watch the bad ones.

If you're a fan of Westerns, you can probably turn back now. Not only have you probably seen the original 1960 version of "The Magnificent Seven," but you've probably seen "Seven Samurai." If that's the case, the only reason for watching this movie is if you're a Denzel Washington completist like me. Again, this isn't because the movie is bad; it's just that it doesn't bring anything to the table aside from updating the call sheet.

Here are some of the other Denzel Washington movies I've covered to date in my Denzel Watchathon:

Read more: Every Denzel Watchathon movie so far

But for those of you who are new to the Western genre, this movie is a great starting point. It stars Denzel Washington as Sam Chisholm, a bounty hunter from Kansas. He is in search of a fugitive, which causes him to cross paths with Emma (Haley Bennett). Her town of Rose Creek is in danger of being taken by a mining tycoon, Bartholomew Bogue (Peter Sarsgaard), and she's trying to find men who will help the townspeople keep their land.

Sam agrees to help her, so he recruits a group of six others (Chris Pratt, Ethan Hawke, Vincent D'Onofrio, Lee Byung-hun, Manuel Garcia-Rulfo and Martin Sensmeier) to help Emma and her fellow citizens make a stand against Bogue. Upon arrival in Rose Creek, they quickly dispatch Bogue's agents left behind to keep the townspeople in check, then get to work training the newly conscripted soldiers and setting up traps for when Bogue eventually returns to claim the town.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

The 1960 original and Kurosawa's 1954 classic both make serious attempts to emotionally connect you to the seven soldiers and the townspeople they're defending. There are romantic entanglements, class struggles, betrayals and more. This 2016 version only ever hints at delving deeper into its characters.

That's essentially the long and short of this movie's plot, aside from the climactic battle, and that is one of the problems with this remake.

The 1960 original and Kurosawa's 1954 classic both make serious attempts to emotionally connect you to the seven soldiers and the townspeople they're defending. There are romantic entanglements, class struggles, betrayals and more.

This 2016 version only ever hints at delving deeper into its characters. We learn that Ethan Hawke's Goodnight Robicheaux is suffering from PTSD following his time as a Confederate sharpshooter in the U.S. Civil War, which Chris Pratt's Joshua Faraday refers to as "The War of Northern Aggression." Given that both these men are currently serving under Sam, a Black man — Sam and Robicheaux even clearly have a strong friendship — you'd think that there'd be some deeper exploration of the racial tensions between these characters. But clearly Robicheaux and Faraday were just fighting for states' rights.

The closest we really get to any sort of emotional connection is in the film's climactic battle, when Faraday and Vasquez (Manuel Garcia-Rulfo), who openly harbor some dislike for each other over racial animosity, find themselves bonded by blood in the movie's deadlier moments. We also learn, just at the end, why Sam really agreed to help these townspeople. This is a construct crafted completely by the 2016 movie, so I won't spoil the big reveal for you here. But I'll just say I wish the movie had chosen to let us sit with the information going into the final gunfight rather than turn it into a twist.

Still, if you're brand new to Westerns, the final showdown of this movie is undeniably epic. It's around 30 minutes of non-stop shooting and action. So, if you're just looking to dip your toe into the genre, it's okay if you pick something like this that doesn't quite get into the nuances and emotional depth that it should. Start with this, see if you can handle the violence, and then branch out into more well-crafted movies in the genre.

It's worth noting that my recommendation of this as a starter Western is only applicable as long as this movie is streaming for free. I cannot and do not advocate that you pay any of your hard-earned dollars to see this film. Not when there are other great Westerns available to stream for free, including "Seven Samurai," which, despite its setting of feudal Japan, is undeniably a Western.

But that movie is also in Japanese, so it's less accessible for those not yet fully comfortable with subtitles. It's also in black-and-white, which isn't everyone's preferred format. For me, someone who owns the Criterion Collection 4K disc of Kurosawa's masterpiece, those aspects of the film are both positives, but I can see how they'd be barriers to entry.

So if you're truly just looking for a starter-kit Western, you could do a lot worse than Denzel's version of "The Magnificent Seven," and not least of all because it has Denzel in it. Just make sure, once you're done, to branch out further into the genre. Once you're hooked, there's an entire lifetime's worth of Western movies to explore.

Stream "The Magnificent" for free right now on Tubi or for free on YouTube

The Euro 2022 winner is a part of Gary Neville’s The Overlap show

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

During her playing career, Jill Scott was never one to back out of a challenge.

The Euro 2022 winner won a slew of major honours with Everton and Manchester City, but can now be found getting stuck in with the likes of Roy Keane, Gary Neville, Ian Wright and Jamie Carragher in front of the camera for The Overlap.

The Lionesses legend quickly became a staple of the successful YouTube channel, giving as good as she gets with the gaggle of former Premier League stars.

Scott admits that the atmosphere around the table can get so relaxed that she can sometimes forget she’s appearing in front of an audience. Rather than feeling like another television commitment, getting together with her co-presenters is more like a catch-up with old friends.

“I actually forget that people are watching and listening – I’m just excited because I haven’t seen the guys for a couple of weeks,” she tells FourFourTwo.

“I’m like, ‘Let’s have your opinions on football’, but it’s just like chatting with your mates. I love it.”

Scott believes that this chemistry between the team, and the panel’s different backgrounds, have been key to the show’s success.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

“All of our relationships are different,” she continues. “I’d describe it as like siblings in a way. I like that they’ve got big personalities, because that’s when they get into arguments.

“But what I love, and I think it’s their generation, is that you can say something that someone doesn’t agree with, but then walk away and still be the best of mates. That’s how football should be.

“When I played, you could be playing against your best mate in an FA Cup final and one of you would win, then the next day you’d go back to being best friends.”

And while Scott is quick to embrace the friendly arguments and differences of opinion in the studio, she can’t help but think back to her playing days, and believes that the women’s game’s reputation for being less confrontational than its male counterparts is wide of the mark.

“People think that in women’s football we didn’t have banter, but I actually think it’s more ruthless than in men’s football,” she insists. “I could tell you plenty of stories from my playing days, but they wouldn’t make it into the magazine! If you don’t know someone and they’re saying things to try to bring you down, then it crosses into bullying.

“But we’re friends, and I give it back to them just as much. I actually feel like I get more leeway sometimes, being female. I love that they feel relaxed and anything that’s said, I always know it’s in jest.”

For more than a decade, Joe Mewis has worked in football journalism as a reporter and editor. Mewis has had stints at Mirror Football and LeedsLive among others and worked at FourFourTwo throughout Euro 2024, reporting on the tournament. In addition to his journalist work, Mewis is also the author of four football history books that include times on Leeds United and the England national team. Now working as a digital marketing coordinator at Harrogate Town, too, Mewis counts some of his best career moments as being in the iconic Spygate press conference under Marcelo Bielsa and seeing his beloved Leeds lift the Championship trophy during lockdown.

Star Wars is experiencing a golden age of video games

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The movies might be struggling to recapture the magic, but Star Wars video games are strong with the Force.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After a tumultuous start to EA's rebooted Star Wars Battlefront series, in 2021 Lucasfilm and Disney decided to open up the development of new Star Wars games by bringing in different publishers and studios under the Lucasfilm Games banner. Now, over five years on, we can confidently say we're entering a new golden era of amazing Star Wars games.

With LucasArts' successor calling the shots and coordinating a multitude of projects set in a galaxy far, far away, even EA's latest efforts like Squadrons and the Jedi series have taken bigger and bolder swings. Now, in 2026, the full potential of the Star Wars universe has become clearer with specific releases squarely aimed at niche audiences.

It's become clear that future Star Wars games needn't be expensive endeavors full of familiar faces or concepts. Star Wars Zero Company and Galactic Racer's restrained scope and confidence have been infectious, and, as it turns out, they're already recognized by all the parties involved as double-A success stories.

A Burnout-inspired Star Wars racer (created by a bunch of ex-Burnout devs) is a fantastic pitch if you know your video games, but looking at EA's recent and current direction, it's easy to see why it didn't happen under their reign. The original Burnout studio, Criterion, would have been an excellent candidate, but instead they were sent to work in the Battlefield mines. Still, someone was interested in the idea, and the end result is glorious.

Fast and furious, Star Wars: Galactic Racer ignores recent racing game trends and bets big on arcade-y vibes, uncomplicated controls, and a high skill ceiling that challenges you with extremely high speeds, tight turns, and unexpected interruptions as other drivers try to ruin your day.

The not-so-secret sauce, however, was its runs-based structure, which injects the flexibility and replay value of roguelite games into the more traditional arcade racing structure that older gamers are familiar with.

Fully confident in its central gameplay idea and devoid of modern live-service baggage, Fuse Games' debut title makes the most of the Star Wars setting within the restrictions of simply making a fun and engaging Star Wars racer.

It's almost the same approach we felt in Zero Company, a turn-based tactical strategy-RPG that comes from the minds of another team of ex-triple-A developers. "Star Wars but XCOM" should've happened earlier, but we're glad it did this year, and, unsurprisingly, it's a banger.

The correct read of their success should be obvious (and so far, it appears the powers that be are seeing it): Let seasoned and fresh developers cook games they're excited about.

Big, medium, or small, players will react positively to experiences that really feel genuine and aren't brought down by tacked-on systems or monetization. Ubisoft's Massive Entertainment also got it right with Star Wars Outlaws, but that ambitious open-world title — hardly a fresh pitch nowadays — also faced high sales expectations due to an inevitably gigantic production budget.

Allowing Star Wars to get weirder and more specific could also help the movie and TV side of things. We know Star Wars has finally locked in clearer big-screen plans for the coming years, and with Disney Plus TV series being seemingly scaled down and targeting more distinct stories better suited for a non-theatrical medium, the crowds' responses to Lucasfilm Games' wins shouldn't be overlooked.

You can't manufacture a viral character like Biddy Blas, arguably Star Wars' most popular new character since Grogu's debut in 2019. It just happens. And it happens naturally if Star Wars is allowed to explore the weird corners of a galaxy.

Devoid of ties to the larger canon and set away from the Imperial era, Galactic Racer makes the post-war period richer by going smaller. It's a reminder of much of the promise introduced by The Mandalorian's first season, before it became distractingly "relevant" to the larger saga.

Even the Clone Wars-set Zero Company keeps things relatively low-profile. Yes, major characters like Anakin Skywalker make cameos, but they don't rob the A-Team and Suicide Squad-inspired tale of camaraderie and subterfuge of its valuable independence. Sure, hits like Andor and Rogue One were obvious inspirations for its tone, but the story stands alone, allowing even Star Wars newcomers to jump in and enjoy it with minimal context.

At a time when giant decades-old franchises often struggle to recruit new fans, the last two Star Wars games reinforce the property's new direction in a medium that can reach more potential Padawans than any other.

The good trick? Just allowing Star Wars to be fresh and smaller — and with no homework required — from time to time. In turn, the larger stories on the horizon will be stronger with the Force.

Command an elite squad in a gritty, authentic Star Wars story set in the twilight of the Clone Wars. Step into the boots of Hawks, a former Republic officer leading Zero Company on a covert operation against an emerging threat that could consume the galaxy.

Available on PS5, Xbox Series X|S, and PC.

How Elizabeth Taylor’s Childhood and Career Were Defined by Her Overbearing Mother

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Elizabeth Taylor, like many other actors of her time, experienced the demands of Hollywood from a very young age. Her parents could never have imagined on February 27, 1932, just how much their newborn baby would achieve artistically. Sooner rather than later, however, they recognized her potential. Although she spent most of her life in Southern California in the heart of the movie industry, Taylor was born in Hampstead, England, to an American family consisting of her father, Francis Lenn Taylor, her mother, Sara Sothern, and their son, Howard, the movie star’s brother with whom she was close. They didn’t remain in Great Britain long, returning to the United States in 1939, shortly before World War II.

Sara Sothern with her children, Elizabeth and Howard Taylor.

The family sailed from London to New York and then settled in Los Angeles. By then, Taylor already had some experience in the performing arts, as she had been taking ballet lessons since she was just three years old. She made her debut in a recital that same year. Once in California, the father of the violet-eyed legend opened an art gallery featuring works imported from the UK. This would turn out to be an important step that brought the Taylors into the world of Hollywood. As biographer Alexander Walker would recount in Elizabeth: The Life of Elizabeth Taylor (Grove Press), the space “opened many doors for the Taylors, leading them directly into the society of money and prestige.”

Despite their differences, mother and daughter grew closer as they matured.

Sothern, the mother of this acting prodigy, saw her daughter as the perfect excuse to live the dream she didn’t fulfill because she was focused on caring for her family. If she couldn’t pursue an acting career, her daughter would. Seeing the effect that Taylor’s beauty had on others, Sothern set out to introduce her to people in the film industry. “Elizabeth Taylor probably wouldn’t have been an actress if it hadn’t been for her mother,” notes actor George Hamilton in the BBC documentary Elizabeth Taylor: Rebel Superstar. In this same documentary, Liz herself can be heard saying in an old recording: “My mother was an actress, but then she married my father, and he didn’t want her to be one, so she gave it up. I think she lived her life through me.”

Kate Andersen Brower, author of Elizabeth Taylor: The Grit and Glamour of an Icon (Harper Collins), explains that Sothern was behind the scenes trying to control every aspect of her daughter’s life in order to make her famous. This included sending Taylor to the same schools as the children of movie studio executives. Soon, thanks to a meeting with the fiancée of a high-ranking Universal Pictures exec who visited the gallery run by Taylor’s father, Elizabeth made her big-screen debut. Her early Hollywood years were propelled by connections her mother painstakingly built.

In 1949, Elizabeth Taylor and Sara Sothern pose at their home in Beverly Hills.

Taylor’s first movie was There’s One Born Every Minute, filmed when she was only 10 years old. Also starring Carl Switzer, who played Alfalfa in Our Gang, it was not a success, and Universal chose not to extend her option. Andersen Brower quotes the studio’s casting director: “The kid has nothing. Her eyes are too old; she doesn’t have the face of a kid.” Sara persisted, however, and through her husband, she met Sam Marx, a producer working on an MGM film, Lassie Come Home. Taylor would be cast in a small role.

Elizabeth Taylor and her mother in a 1948 photo taken in London.

Its success paved the way for another milestone in Taylor’s early career. At age 12, she landed the role of Velvet Brown in National Velvet, alongside Mickey Rooney and Angela Lansbury. Released in December 1944, it grossed almost $6 million worldwide, making it one of MGM’s top films of that period. Taylor signed a new contract with the studio, though she later complained about how she was treated by its head, Louis B. Mayer.

Elizabeth Taylor helps her mother prepare hot dogs and hamburgers at their home in Los Angeles in 1947.

At the same time, her father was both astonished by and jealous of his teenage daughter’s success. An alcoholic and physically abusive, on one occasion he punched her in the jaw, and for the rest of her life, she suffered from TMJ, or lockjaw. Andersen Brower writes, “She explained it away—as she always had—saying that her father was embarrassed that Taylor was the breadwinner of the family and that he felt emasculated.”

Sothern was a practicing Christian (though she was also interested in Judaism, which may have contributed to Taylor’s decision to convert to that religion so that she could marry Eddie Fisher, her fourth husband), and she taught her daughter from an early age that she could not sleep with any man until she was married to him. It’s a view that Taylor appears to have adopted: “Nothing comes off until the ring goes on,” she told gossip columnist Louella Parsons. Sothern, however, had her doubts. Before Taylor married Conrad “Nicky” Hilton in 1950, when she was just 18 years old, Sothern paid a doctor to confirm that her daughter was still a virgin and also to open her hymen to spare her pain during the first time she had sex.

Years later, Taylor would admit that her early years in the film industry were not easy. Her parents spent practically all the money she earned as a child actor. In a 1997 interview with Barbara Walters on 20/20, she described her childhood as “lonely.” She once confided to her close friend George Hamilton that she just wanted “to be a little girl again,” to relive those years that she never got to enjoy.

Originally published by Vanity Fair Spain.

The Fridge-Hacking Spree Is an Internet-of-Things Wake-Up Call

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Welcome to Kernel Panic, a weekly newsletter by Lily Hay Newman and Matt Burgess from inside the new world of privacy and digital security. To receive this newsletter in your inbox each week, sign up here.

Amid an onslaught of AI vulnerability discovery and AI-assisted hacking, some of the most consequential critical infrastructure attacks this year may have little to do with the technology at all—instead providing a stark reminder that cybersecurity fundamentals are still key to digital defense.

Three sites in France that handle blood and plasma bags were briefly impacted this week by widespread attacks on vulnerable internet-connected refrigerators. On Wednesday, the Établissement Français du Sang (EFS) confirmed that cold rooms storing blood were “raised to very high temperatures” on November 2. The blood samples, thankfully, weren’t altered or damaged, the EFS said, because the organization’s alarm facilities and staff quickly detected the changes and implemented fail-safe measures.

The incident, however, comes amid a wave of cyberattacks in recent days affecting supermarket chains in at least 11 European countries that also drastically increased the temperatures of their refrigerators. Pictures from across the continent show huge volumes of food being trashed following the spikes. Similar fridge attacks in October affected hundreds of French stores that were forced to throw away food. And in August, roughly six commissaries and grocery stores on United States military bases suffered similar incidents resulting in spoiled food. The Pentagon is investigating the incidents and is collaborating with French law enforcement, Reuters reported last week.

“It's been true for more than a decade now that our dependence on connected technology is growing faster than our collective will to protect it, which has exposed us to accidents, adversaries, and now potentially agents,” says longtime critical infrastructure researcher Josh Corman, who is currently the executive in residence for public safety and resilience at the nonprofit Institute for Security and Technology. “These fragile cold-chain systems are too critical to fail, but we keep experiencing hard lessons.”

It is still unclear who is behind the incidents and whether the attacks are all connected or being carried out by multiple actors. The first recent wave of attacks against French supermarkets last month appears to have been caused by attackers exploiting unpatched refrigeration controllers exposed to the internet—including software versions that allegedly used weak passwords formulated based on the date.

Many of the hacks stem from attackers exploiting vulnerabilities in temperature controllers made by the Danish firm Danfoss, and researchers say the attacks would not have been particularly difficult to pull off. Danfoss did not return WIRED’s request for comment.

In other statements, Danfoss said impacted systems “were running outdated firmware” and urged customers to keep products updated. In an alert on Thursday addressing the rash of incidents, the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency said attacks could exploit flaws in cold storage systems via authentication bypasses, arbitrary code injections, and weak configurations. Experts have long warned about the dire threats to critical infrastructure posed by these types of weaknesses in so-called internet-of-things devices.

Russian hacktivists tried to claim responsibility for some of the attacks but did not provide credible evidence. Still, some researchers see signs that Russia-based actors may have been involved in at least some of the incidents. The Kremlin this week denied involvement. In other cases, researchers have found potential ties to Iran-linked actors. NextGov/FCW reported last week that unnamed US officials suspect Iran-linked attackers may have been behind the freezer attacks on US military bases, though they noted that the investigation is still ongoing.

This type of refrigerator/freezer attack is not new. Back in 2022, 16 supermarkets in Russia were targeted at the start of the country’s full-scale war with Ukraine. That year, 40 stores in Israel were similarly attacked, according to a 2025 report by Danfoss.

“Many businesses use older technology while the systems become increasingly connected,” Sweden’s National Cyber Security Center pointed out in a recent advisory about the freezer attacks, noting that companies should inventory which of their devices are connected to the internet, disconnect them whenever possible, and make sure they are kept access-controlled, patched, and monitored if they must be connected.

This Is Why Your Trail Cameras Aren’t Working Properly

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 7, 2026 1:00 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

You’ve finally got your trail camera set up in the spot. Every day or two it sends you a photo of a giant old buck, and now all you’ve got to do is wait for the right wind to sneak in there and hunt him. And then, all of a sudden, the camera goes dead.

It’s a new camera, the battery life seemed fine, cell reception was decent … What the hell? Welcome to the often frustrating world of modern trail camera use.

I’ve been field testing and running trail cameras year-round for several years now and in my experience, there is no camera brand out there that’s immune to malfunctions and failures. But my experience pales in comparison to that of Rich Howell of Trail Cam Pro. Howell has been objectively testing and experimenting with trail cameras for more than 20 years.

I recently interviewed Howell on the Outdoor Life podcast and he shared several reasons why hunters see so many camera failures in the field. Some of it has to do with how we’re using the cameras, and some of it has to do with how the cams are made (which is usually pretty cheaply). Plus, Howell hints at where he thinks the future of trail cameras is headed, and it’s all about making a system that is more affordable and useful to hunters. It’s a conversation worth listening to ahead of the heart of hunting season.

In the meantime, here’s why our cameras often go down in the field, and what we can do to fix them.

Standard alkaline batteries often don’t have the required power to keep a trail camera running reliably, Howell says. If you’re getting fine daytime pictures, but blank, black images at night, it’s a good sign you have a battery issue as there’s not enough power for the camera to fire the flash. This can happen even when the camera is displaying decent battery life.

More expensive lithium batteries can work well, but they too can have issues. Howell noticed that about 1 in 20 lithium batteries would go bad very quickly, (like within days of use) and sometimes this was enough to prevent the camera from working.

Howell now suggests that hunters use rechargeable lithium batteries, which have proven to be more reliable. Having a pool of these batteries on rotation will help solve many trail camera power troubles.

When hunters first jump into the cellular trail camera game, they often like to get their photos sent immediately. In the beginning it’s fun to get a notification and then have that instant gratification of seeing a deer photo.

But it’s usually more effective to have a cell camera batch send images every 12 hours or 24 hours. This is a key way to preserve battery life, and it’s also a more useful way to analyze trail camera data.

Personally, I like to have cameras send every 24 hours and then I review all the cameras everyday in the morning. Looking at all the cameras on the property at once helps me put together activity trends.

Howell says that solar panels definitely can help keep your camera powered, but they often have issues in the field. First, you want to make sure that they are facing south to capture as most sun as possible. This can be a challenge if you want your camera facing north. And second, squirrels have a tendency to chew through solar panel wires. If you’re setting up your camera and panel in the hardwoods, there’s a good chance a squirrel is going to get to it. In other words, solar panels can be a useful tool, but they’re not a silver bullet.

If you’re using the stock camera strap, you’re just asking for trouble. The tag ends of these straps always seem to come loose (triggering the camera) and you’re never able to position the camera perfectly on the tree with them. Plus, other hunters can easily spot the strap, which is an important consideration if you’re hunting public land. Howell suggests using an adjustable tree mount to make sure you can position the camera exactly where it needs to go. You can find a wealth of  trail camera information on the Trail Cam Pro website and by subscribing to their newsletter.

Alex Robinson is Outdoor Life’s editor-in-chief. He oversees an ace team of writers, photographers, and editors who are scattered across the continent and cover everything from backcountry sheep hunting to trail running.

Learn more about Outdoorlife.com Editorial Standards

Rank Data Problems, PageSpeed Scores & AI Training Opt-Out – SEO Pulse via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Rank data may carry one-word results and redirect URLs. Other updates cover PageSpeed lab scores, a shared AI training opt-out, technical SEO and AI search.

Welcome to the week’s Pulse: what you need to know about updates that are affecting how much you can rely on rank tracking data, why PageSpeed scores can go up or down on a page that has no changes and what a shared robots.txt control would mean in terms of preferences for AI training.

Here’s what matters for you and your work.

Two issues in the Google results that rank tracking tools collect can throw off ranking reports. Unrelated listings can crowd a tracked page out of the collected results, and unresolved goto links can hide which page ranked.

In an SE Ranking review of 10,000 Google US desktop keywords published Nov. 3, 84 percent of the flagged cases were unrelated listings about a single word from the query. Of those unrelated blocks, 96.5% began at position 9 or below. A related pattern is tracked by Nozzle using a different methodology and it reports its weekly share of impacted SERPs had fallen to 1.5% in mid-October, then rose to 7.6% during the week of Oct. 26. DataForSEO states Google has implemented a mechanism to restrict the amount of goto links from a single results page that can be resolved over a given timeframe. As such, they state their High Priority and Live modes will now only resolve approximately half of goto URLs. The status page of Serper lists the limitation of goto URLs as being on Oct. 30, while SerpApi’s second incident of goto links replacing direct links, which opened on Nov. 3, still showed as Identified when last reviewed on Nov. 4.

Check if you were dropped at a position of 9 or lower from late September on to see if your replacement URLs are about one word of your query. Also, starting with about Oct. 30 (if it was a Google redirect) compare the URL that your tracker stored versus where the redirect points. To verify which factor caused an issue use Search Console’s impression/clicks reports for the same query, as SE Ranking recommends.

Read our full coverage: Rank Trackers Find Google Results About One Word Of A Query and SERP Data Providers Say Google Is Throttling Goto Result Links

PageSpeed Insights now runs Lighthouse 13.6.0, which updates the loading simulation used for its lab scores. As a result, your page’s score may change even if you haven’t changed anything on the page.

According to a post by Google’s Barry Pollard on Bluesky, the updated version of Lighthouse’s simulated FCP (First Contentful Paint) and LCP (Largest Contentful Paint) will be more accurate and will generally provide better results. He also stated that JavaScript-intensive websites would be more likely to experience the impact of this new version. The simulation used as the basis for these predictions, called Lantern, has been modified to use Chrome’s render-blocking label for every individual request made. It then defaults to using the fetch priority value if the render-blocking label cannot be found. In addition, the field data provided in the same report was based upon actual user data collected from the real world, not as part of a synthetic test like Lighthouse.

The pre- and post-switch lab scores aren’t generated in the same simulation; therefore, some of the increase you see on pages you’ve optimized might be from the new version. Make sure each report lists the same Lighthouse version, and compare your field data, before claiming an improvement.

Read our full coverage: Google Lighthouse Update Changes How PageSpeed Insights Simulates Loading

Google, Microsoft, and OpenAI announced on Oct. 30 that they plan to honor a shared robots.txt control for AI training preferences, based on an Internet-Draft by Anthropic’s Kevin Kelley.

The control is a normal robots.txt group under a policy token called __usage_ai-training, that has both Allow & Disallow rules; at this time none of these three organizations support the control, according to their publicly announced timelines. Google’s post states that it plans to treat a shared opt-out as though a site opted out of Google-Extended, when the site does not have Google-Extended defined. In addition, Google’s crawler documentation states that Google-Extended also covers grounding in Gemini Apps. Microsoft will utilize the shared control for training purposes, will introduce a separate page-level AI Grounding preference, and will cease treating NoArchive and NoCache as training and/or grounding preferences after March 1, 2027.

A site would be able to use one robots.txt group as replacement for many per-company training rules after the companies read it; however, opting out through that group would not cover the same uses at Google and Microsoft. The documentation provided by Google regarding its robots.txt controls does not separate Gemini training from Gemini Apps’ grounding. Companies using NoArchive and/or NoCache in conjunction with Microsoft have until March 1, 2027 to switch to the new preferences.

Read our full coverage: Google, Microsoft & OpenAI Plan To Honor Shared AI Training Opt-Out

Google’s John Mueller said AI systems “build on SEO” and that he expects technical SEO in particular to stay relevant. He spoke with David Iwanow in an interview posted Oct. 29 from Search Central Deep Dive Europe 2026.

Mueller also stated that AI systems crawl sites in slightly different ways using intent based on things like training versus getting the latest content; he described this as “another flavor of technical SEO”. When asked about listicle formats Mueller indicated that when tactics are effective they are likely to be mimicked poorly until the algorithms adapt and therefore establishing a solid foundation for a website is your best bet.

Mueller placed AI crawling within technical SEO, so you can include how AI crawlers request your pages in the technical audits you already run. Just be sure to check each crawler’s access and robots.txt rules individually.

Read our full coverage: Google Says Technical SEO Remains Key For AI SEO

ChatGPT consumer product lead Adam Fry told Big Technology that ChatGPT’s “volume of search-like queries is now roughly one-fifth the volume of queries of Google”.

Fry noted each question that sends ChatGPT to the web is considered a search by ChatGPT’s developer; however, according to the article, there is no Google figure, period or market given for this comparison. Ahrefs’ referral tracker measures the source of referrals at the site level and shows Google accounts for 24.74% of tracked traffic across 118,671 sites during October with ChatGPT accounting for 0.36%.

OpenAI’s ratio counts questions that send ChatGPT to the web, while referral data tracks visits sent to websites. To keep your channel plan clear, name the measure behind any ChatGPT size figure, because search counts and referral shares describe different things.

Read our full coverage: Google’s Search Volume Is About 5x ChatGPT’s, OpenAI Says

A number of statistics reported by SEO teams this week require an assessment of how these statistics are collected prior to being viewed as information regarding performance for a website or channel. This week’s issue with rank data is the collection of one word results and redirect URLs; a simulated PageSpeed lab score will be affected when using a new simulation versus the previous simulation; and the size of ChatGPT will depend upon if you use search volume or total visits to determine its size. On the other hand, the planned shared opt-out would cover different uses based upon which company reads it; Mueller defined AI crawling, with its different intents, as technical SEO. When reporting changes in metrics or data from reports, this is where you should document which tracking tool or version of a tool was used as well as the criteria used (definition) to produce the report.
