Bitcoin Price Prediction: Can BTC Reclaim $82,000 as ETF Inflows Slip to $56 Billion?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Can Bitcoin climb back to $82,000? A recent forecast says BTC must reclaim that level before any real recovery, even as cumulative spot Bitcoin ETF inflows slip to about $56 billion.

That makes the next few weeks pivotal for any Bitcoin price prediction.

BTC trades in a narrow range well below its record high, and a possible Fed rate cut is the catalyst traders now watch.

Traders who position for that move also keep an eye on early-stage projects.

One of them is MEMASA ($MEMASA), an upcoming AI trading app that lets users research and trade crypto through a chat.

Bitcoin has drifted lower for a month, and a recent 24/7 Wall St analysis says the key test now sits near $82,000.BTC needs a sustained close above that level, the analysis argues, before any real recovery can take hold.

The main reason is weak fund flows, as cumulative spot Bitcoin ETF inflows have slipped to about $56 billion. Only a couple of weeks earlier, that total was higher, which means large investors have been taking money out rather than adding.

Monetary policy offers little help either, since the Fed’s benchmark rate recently moved up instead of down. Still, the analysis sees a return above $100,000 as plausible within the next couple of years, if ETF demand and Fed cuts line up.

Bitcoin now trades near $76,000, down almost 9% over the past month after a steady slide.

Nearest strong support sits near $73,500, and technical indicators lean bearish, so a retest of that floor looks likely.

For any near-term Bitcoin price prediction, losing that floor could open a path toward $70,000.

A move above resistance near $84,400, backed by returning ETF inflows, would change the picture for bulls.

MEMASA is an upcoming AI crypto trading app that lets users analyze markets, check token risks, and trade on-chain by chatting with an AI assistant.

The app tackles problems familiar to crypto traders: too many charts, scattered market data, scam tokens, and complicated interfaces. MEMASA plans to combine market intelligence, risk checks, automated strategies, and trading in one self-custodial app.

If the crypto market enters another bullish phase, growing interest in altcoins could bring more people into trading. MEMASA aims to serve that audience by making market research and trading as simple as a conversation.

Its potential appeal extends from beginners struggling with trading interfaces to experienced traders who want faster access to market insights.

$MEMASA is the utility token powering the MEMASA ecosystem. Its planned uses include premium AI intelligence, trading fee discounts, marketplace payments, and staking for analysts and AI agents.

The token’s potential derives from stronger sentiment across the crypto market and growing demand within the MEMASA ecosystem. If the app attracts a wide audience, it might drive the demand for $MEMASA.

MEMASA is currently offering early access to $MEMASA through an on-chain early sale, with the present stage set at $0.0155, below the project’s anticipated public listing price. Earlier stages offer lower prices, while later stages provide faster token unlocks.

A Bitcoin rally could create favorable conditions for MEMASA’s debut. The bigger test will be whether the app delivers on its promise to simplify crypto trading and attracts enough users to build lasting demand for its token.

Bitcoin’s next move hinges on whether buyers defend support near $73,500. A push back above $82,000, with ETF inflows returning, would give bulls their first real win.

BTC has still more than doubled over three years, so this slump follows a long climb. A Fed rate cut remains the catalyst to watch.

MEMASA faces a different test, as it needs to turn an AI trading concept into an app people use regularly. If it succeeds, demand for its utility token could grow with the ecosystem. Both Bitcoin’s path and MEMASA’s outcome remain uncertain, and higher potential comes with higher risk.

We’d Kill to See These <i><em>MobLand </em></i>Spin-Offs

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

With season 2 racing toward its finale and season 3 still some way off, we put our imagination to work.

If ever there were a case for “if something’s worth doing, it’s worth overdoing,” it’s MobLand. I don’t know who first said it—someone impersonating Guy Ritchie, probably.

Of all Ritchie’s gangland offspring, MobLand sits at the very tip—the tip of the iceberg, tip of the tongue, anonymous tip. It has already been tipped for awards this season.

Season 3 is understood to be in production, due sometime in late 2027. But as the second outing hurtles towards its finale this month, the wait looks positively Homeric. So, in the spirit of overdoing, here are a few spin-offs nobody asked for but richly deserve.

Newgate, the early 2000s. Kevin is a broken young man after an assault by a mean prison guard called Rusby (remember him? Kevin kills him in revenge in MobLand season 1), and he is one bad night from hanging himself with a bedsheet. His cellmate Harry, a quiet new arrival with a thousand-yard stare, talks him down. Then he does something tougher and kinder: he decides Kevin is going to live, whether Kevin likes it or not.

What follows is a double act forged in a concrete box. Harry fights, bargains and bluffs his way up the wing’s pecking order, while Kevin learns to be a person again, ultimately becoming his gobby, hapless sidekick. Between the long, dark stretches in their cell come the scams, the contraband and the beatings. A snooker table in the rec room becomes a secret casino; a rival lag turns out to be a secret snitch; a posh fraudster inmate teaches Harry to talk like an Etonian. Kevin finds his voice as a late-night prison-radio DJ.

Slowly, we watch Harry being hammered into the man that Conrad will later admire. By the final episode, he’s out, and ready to join the Paras.

I’ve made it no secret that Frankie Cooper is my favorite TV villain of 2026. But I’ll only accept a spin-off if Johnny Flynn stays in the role. So…

South London, the 1990s. Young Frankie Cooper is a gofer at a scrapyard, a well-manicured apprentice crook with an unfashionable hunger: he wants to know things. He enrolls in night classes, haunts the mobile library, and devours encyclopedias. Every lesson becomes a weapon. A lecture on Napoleon teaches him tactics. A documentary on parasites teaches him patience. Sun Tzu teaches him war.

Meanwhile he assembles his crew from the yard’s misfits: a weeping mechanic, a seven-foot pigeon fancier and a man who speaks solely in historical metaphors. Each week Frankie cons a bigger fish, charming the mark and leaving a trail of crushed-car cubes dripping blood. Beneath the bonhomie, we watch a very amiable monster being assembled, limb by limb, lesson by lesson, murder by murder.

Between all this, there's a love story—he meets the woman who will become Michael's mother. She's a nail technician from the posh part of Sydenham. She teaches him cuticle care, foot hygiene. Then she’s killed by a stray bullet in a shootout, leaving Frankie with a newborn son and a thirst for vengeance.

By the finale, the scrap yard is an empire. Then comes the one subject he can’t revise for: single fatherhood.

Ten years after MobLand, the Harrigans are all dead. Kat MacAllister won the war, salted the earth, and walked into the White House as president of the United States. But she never forgot that Harry turned her down. He survived the war, and lives off-grid on a remote Scottish isle, tending sheep and reading Plato. Once a month, he gets a postcard from Gina who is studying a master’s degree in Comparative Criminal Justice at The Hague.

One day, Harry receives a strange postcard from Gina, asking him to come. But something isn’t right. She signs off with “Your hugglepuff, Georgina.” He’s never called her Hugglepuff, and she always hated the name Georgina.

It’s a trap, and Harry knows it. Still, he dusts down his old leather coat and gets a ferry to the mainland. What follows is a race around Europe, from the Hebrides to Belgium to Washington DC, on one last job to save the only person he ever truly loved. On the journey he discovers that Gina’s criminology thesis accidentally unearthed a paper trail linking Kat's old MobLand war chest to the untraceable offshore funds that financed her presidential campaign.

Kat sees an opportunity. If she baits Harry out of the woodwork, she can frame him for a rogue, violent operation in Europe, eliminating both the evidence (Gina) and the last loose end from her past (Harry) in one clean stroke.

The hound is out of retirement. And, this time, it’s presidential.

This one charts Maeve’s humble beginnings in the wild west of Ireland. It follows her early years picking the pockets of drunk farmers at the Galway Races. At a dance, an amorous young man drags Maeve into a bush. What he hasn't considered is the razor blade she keeps in her petticoat. She cuts off his testicles.

She's the youngest and smallest–and by far the smartest—of a clan of bare-knuckle boxer brothers who never think to ask her to a fight.

So she watches and learns. She learns what men do when they think no one is watching, and what they’ll pay to keep it quiet. Her first scheme is small and sly. Her tenth ends with a bookie in a bog and the Irish mob on her tail.

She heads to London where she meets a young pickpocket with a bad mustache and a worse temper, and she sees the perfect partner. His name is Conrad Harrigan. Together they murder every last gangster with a grudge (and there are many).

It’s a love story, of a kind. Two outlaws, one getaway car, and a trail of widows in their rearview mirror.

The Term ‘Medical-Grade Skincare’ is Confusing. Here’s What You Need to Know.

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Is it the key to a healthier complexion or just a gimmick? Experts weigh in.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Somewhere between the serum shelf and your For You page, “medical-grade” became the most reassuring phrase in beauty. It conjures authoritative images of a lab coat, a prescription pad, and the idea that your money is going somewhere serious. But, plot twist: Not one person regulates the term. “The phrase ‘medical-grade’ is vague and arbitrary,” dermatologist Joshua Zeichner, MD, says bluntly. “It does not correlate with effectiveness or safety.”

And consumers are starting to catch on. After all, there are only a few skincare ingredients proven to work, and dermatologist Mona Gohara, MD, says skeptical shoppers ask her about the term every day. “My patients and followers are wondering, ‘is this skincare backed by science, and will it work?’ They want to know whether they’re investing in healthier skin or just a prettier bottle,” she says. After all, no one wants to be duped by products and ingredients that are a total scam.

Meet the experts: Joshua Zeichner, MD, is an associate professor of dermatology and the director of cosmetic & clinical research in dermatology at Mount Sinai Hospital in New York City. Mona Gohara, MD, is a clinical professor in the department of dermatology at Yale School of Medicine.

So, let’s sort the science from the sales pitch. Read on to find out what the term (kinda, sorta) means, what it doesn’t, and how to shop the skincare aisle like a dermatologist.

Ask around, and the definitions of ‘”medical-grade” skincare multiply. Dr. Zeichner says it usually indicates a product with a high concentration of science-backed active ingredients, but not always. Depending on the brand, it can refer to the quality of a product’s ingredients, any clinical testing on the final formula, or the use of one particular active.

Dr. Gohara likens the phrase to physician-dispensed skincare designed to complement dermatologic care (in other words, the products you’d purchase at a doctor’s office). But now, she says it became shorthand for formulas built around specific skin concerns and that its visibility has grown as consumers have become more interested in the ingredients and science behind their routines.

Shoppers tend to assume these products are stronger and come with a dermatologist’s guidance, but they shouldn’t, says Dr. Zeichner. In his view, the skincare market is extremely crowded, and this is a way brands are chasing a competitive edge. In some cases, the term is used interchangeably with other unregulated phrases, like “dermatologist-formulated” or “dermatologist-approved,” giving the appearance of a professional quality product, whatever that means.

Let’s start with the regulatory facts. “Medical-grade” is not an FDA-recognized term, Dr. Gohara confirms. In fact, the agency only very loosely regulates the claims of cosmetics. They do draw a line between a cosmetic product and a pharmaceutical (like prescription tretinoin, for example): Cosmetics, which include most moisturizers and serums, are meant to improve the appearance of skin. They can’t claim to change skin’s structure or function.

Over-the-counter drugs, such as acne treatments, sunscreens, and eczema treatments, contain active ingredients cleared by the FDA for a specific condition and their packaging includes a drug facts box listing the active ingredients. This part’s important: The term “medical-grade” does not indicate that a product is an OTC drug. “Slapping it on a label doesn’t give a product a separate regulatory status,” says Dr. Gohara.

The bottom line is that “if you see the term on a label, take it with a big grain of salt,” advises Dr. Zeichner. He says the definition “can mean whatever the brand wants it to.” While the products may have higher concentrations of actives, there are no specific percentages shoppers can look for. Dr. Gohara says that many physician-dispensed brands invest in clinical studies, ingredient stability, and delivery systems that help actives work while limiting irritation, but you can’t always tell from the label alone. “The value comes from that research, formulation, and guidance,” she says. “The term alone doesn’t tell you which advantages a product offers.”

With packaging out of the picture, both doctors point to the same strategy: Start with what your skin needs, then pick ingredients with a proven track record of addressing those concerns. To that end, Dr. Zeichner says retinol is perhaps the best-studied ingredient for aging skin. It’s a form of vitamin A shown to speed cell turnover and stimulate collagen.

He also singles out vitamin C as one to look for. It’s an antioxidant that helps neutralize free radical damage from UV, blocks abnormal pigment production to fade dark spots, and supports collagen production. Lastly, hydroxy acids such as alpha hydroxy acids (think glycolic and lactic) and beta hydroxy acids (salicylic acid) are also worth your dough. The former help dissolve the connections between surface cells so they shed more easily; the latter clears excess oil and helps keep pores clear.

Dr. Gohara names broad-spectrum sunscreen with an SPF 30 or higher as a must-have, a 5% niacinamide serum for uneven tone, and 2.5% benzoyl peroxide for inflamed acne, which she says can work at higher strengths as well (though irritation can happen). She adds that percentages aren’t interchangeable between ingredients, and that the finished formula and consistent use matter, too. If the box doesn’t list ingredient concentrations, Dr. Zeichner suggests snapping a photo of the box and researching the product at home before buying (brand websites often hold a lot of useful information).

And watch for red flags—Dr. Zeichner calls out vague language, a lack of transparency about ingredients or clinical testing, and promises that seem too good to be true. Dr. Gohara says claims to “erase” deep wrinkles, “eliminate” pores, or “clear” years of pigmentation in a weekend are warning signs. “If the promise sounds like a facelift by Friday, skip it.”

SkinBetter Science Alpha Ret Overnight Cream

A dermatologist favorite, Skin Better Science’s famed retinol pairs the vitamin A derivative with lactic acid and hydrators like squalane and ceramides to tackle wrinkles and skin texture with no irritation.

This serum, which is often duplicated, is widely-considered to be the best-of-the-best when it comes to vitamin C products. Pairing the ingredient with ferulic acid and vitamin E (where the product gets its name) keeps the C stable and boosts its effectiveness.

SkinMedica Even & Correct Brightening Treatment Pads

These days, there are more exfoliating pads lining store shelves than there are short-shorts in Harry Styles’ closet. SkinMedica’s are the best—effective, gentle, efficient.

Alastin Skincare HydraTint Pro Mineral Sunscreen SPF 36

Yes, you want an SPF that protects skin from damaging UVA/UVB rays. But this one does so much more: Ectoin shields you from visible blue light while a powerful antioxidant blend keeps free radical damage at bay. This the Medieval armor of your skincare routine.

La Roche-Posay Effaclar Duo Dual Action Acne Cleanser

When it comes to battling breakouts, benzoyl peroxide can’t be beaten. The prob: It can be irritating. A wash is a great solution, killing acne-causing bacteria without being too harsh on skin.

Revision Skincare Advanced Pigment Corrector+

These days, niacinamide serums are a dime a dozen. What makes Revision’s product worth its price is that it pairs this proven pigment fighter with advanced exosomal technology that helps boost skin longevity to stay healthier longer.

So what should your strategy be when shopping for skincare? A few general guidelines:

At the end of the day, the next time you see “medical-grade” on a label, consider it mere marketing copy and keep reading. What’s inside the jar and what the brand can show about the finished product is what matters.

It’s also worth noting that spending more doesn’t buy better results, and more products can mean more problems, advises Dr. Zeichner. Higher concentrations may be necessary in some cases and just cause irritation in others. He keeps regimens simple, with the morning devoted to protection and the evening to hydration and repair. He recommends two products in the morning and two at night for patients. Combining too many can make them less effective and may cause dryness or irritation.

Dr. Gohara also advises saving where you can. An affordable cleanser, moisturizer, and broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher make an excellent foundation. Spending more can be worthwhile for a targeted treatment with research behind the finished formula, such as a stabilized vitamin C serum, a well-formulated retinoid, or a peptide product with evidence for its specific claims. You might also be paying for technology that keeps ingredients stable, makes them easier to tolerate, or gives a more elegant texture. “The upgrade should serve your skin, not just your bathroom shelf,” she says.

Last, know when to skip the shopping. Dr. Gohara says to see a dermatologist for persistent acne, rashes, stubborn pigmentation, or repeated irritation, and promptly for a changing or bleeding spot.

Sometimes expert guidance is the best investment in the routine.

I Have a New Hobby. It’s Kicking My Ass.

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The fastest-growing racquet sport in America may also be the cardio workout I’ve been avoiding.

This column first appeared in the Men’s Health email newsletter. The Optimized Man is a biweekly series from our editorial director as he tries out the latest, greatest, and weirdest health and wellness innovations so you don’t have to. Sign up here to get the next one.

I’VE TRIED TO build a life free of shoulds. I’m fine with woulds and coulds and their vague promise of actionability, but when you get into shoulds, you get hung up on stuff like guilt and doubt and all kinds of second-guessing. I really should work out. I really shouldn’t eat that. You think I should try and sleep more? Thanks, man. Good tip.

Shoulds, put plainly, are a problem, and this year I’ve been staring down two different shoulds that won’t quit staring back: I should probably do more cardio, and I should probably get a hobby. I’ve never been much of a cardio guy or a hobby guy, but with evidence tying both to better physical and mental health outcomes, my list of excuses for not doing either is growing short. And I think I’ve found a solution in one of the fastest-growing sports in America.

It’s called padel (pronounced pa-DEL), and you might’ve come across it if you’ve been on vacation lately in Central America or southern Europe (or somewhere really exotic, like Miami). I discovered it last year on a cold winter’s night, and after playing for two hours on an indoor court and emerging sweaty, sore, and exuberant, I can report that it combines everything I like about tennis, racquetball, and squash into one hard-hitting, heavy-breathing sport.

You can go ahead and YouTube the basics, and here’s the gist: You’re playing on a court that’s about three-quarters the area of a tennis court, with high glass walls along the back, some wire fencing on the sides, and a net down the middle. Instead of pickleball’s slow and endless dinking, a padel ball whirs fast off your midsize racquet (also larger and heavier than pickleball’s), and the action requires you to move. Even though it’s a doubles sport (which typically requires less movement than singles, ‘cause there’s two of you), you’re constantly chasing the ball like it’s that little golden bug in quidditch. Sprint, stop, swing, pivot, repeat. (You can also hit the ball against the back wall to launch it in reverse, back over the net, which requires a massive swing.) The first time I played, I showed up wearing sweatpants and a puffer vest over a T-shirt. Within minutes of starting I had sweat through my T-shirt, and by the end of the first set, I had sweat through the seat of my sweatpants. I looked like John Candy in Splash, post-racquetball. What can I say? I’m not a cardio guy.

And there is a lot of cardio here. Back in 2024 we noticed that tennis had started billing itself as “the world’s healthiest sport,” and we asked writer Nick Pachelli to dig into the branding to see how much of it is hype. According to Nick:

Padel isn’t tennis and tennis isn’t padel, but I’ve been playing tennis for almost four decades, and I’ve never had my ass kicked quite as hard as when I played padel.

Then there is the social aspect of it—you’re part of a pair playing another pair, and whether you’re close friends or total strangers with your partner or opponents, at least you’re not bowling alone. Nick found that when longevity researchers study tennis…

Aerobic exercise, social connection and community, some light competition, and a lot of laughs: Could padel be my new hobby? Could it be my main source of cardio? Winter’s coming and the indoor courts are calling, and with visions of John Candy huffing and puffing in my head, I should probably start training.

Richard Dorment is the editorial director of Men’s Health and Women’s Health.

Exclusive: Robert Aramayo explains the difficulties of playing both Elrond and Elros in season 3

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Rings of Power season 3 sees Elrond star Robert Aramayo play a second role, his Elven character's twin brother Elros. In a flashback sequence in the new season, we finally meet the iconic Lord of the Rings character who was a pivotal part of Middle-earth's history.

In J.R.R. Tolkien's works, the twin brothers were given the same choice by the Valar due to their half-Elven heritage: to decide whether they would want to be Elves or Men. Elrond chooses the immortal path, whereas his brother decides to become a man, going on to be the first king of Númenor.

In season 3, we get to see the implications of this choice in a flashback to the First Age. Speaking to GamesRadar+, Aramayo opens up about finding inspiration for playing the new character.

"I looked at Númenóreans in the show and just thought about what kind of person would make the opposite choice, you know," he explains. "But what's cool about them is that they're so similar. They just go a different way."

Aramayo, who has played Elrond since season one, says he actually found an unexpected part of the scene the most difficult. "What was actually hardest about that scene, I thought, was actually playing Elrond because it was like you had to play a sort of younger, more naive, purer Elrond.

"I made the choice that at that point, whilst he's been through so much in captivity and all of this stuff, I think there's a real purity to Elrond, and it was even stronger back then. So you have to sort of like, in some ways, you're almost playing two new characters because it's like because with Elves they've lived for so long."

The Rings of Power season 3 picks up five years after the events of season 2. The Elves and Sauron are now at war as the Dark Lord's power spreads across Middle-earth. And it seems that unexpected alliances are the only thing that may stop the darkness in the new epic season.

The Rings of Power season 3 begins on November 11 with a four-episode premiere. The remaining episodes release in two-episode chunks on Prime Video.

5 American, 6 Canadian: Workhorse W56 arrives in the True North

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Electric truck brand Workhorse is bringing its W56 Class 5 and 6 electric trucks to Canada thanks to a new agreement with a Canadian dealer group and fleet management provider offering truck and trailer sales, parts, repair, and 24/7 roadside assistance and towing services across the Northern nation.

Building on years of electric step van deployments in Canada and underscoring the company’s commitment to Canada as a growth region, the new dealer agreement with Tristan Fleet Management marks the first-ever Canadian dealer partnership for Workhorse.

“Canadian fleets need proven electric trucks that deliver on quality and performance in any condition,” said James Griffin, Chief Revenue Officer at Workhorse. “The W56 has already proven itself on this front in real-world fleet service across Canada. Tristan’s strong reputation, its extensive network, and ability to provide around-the-clock roadside support will be extremely valuable as Workhorse continues to grow our presence in the country.”

Workhorse has been selling and supporting electric trucks in Canada since 2021 through its partnership with Purolator, a Canadian-owned leading integrated freight, package and logistics solutions provider that has grown alongside the rapid expansion of online shopping for everything from groceries to car parts and road-ready motorcycles has also seen the expansion of last-mile delivery contractors. These fleet operators and drivers are essential workers who keep the online economy rolling, and they are getting positively pummeled by this year’s record fuel prices, a product of America’s latest “dumb oil war” in Iran.

And, after Workhorse’ fleet logged its 20 millionth mile driven with zero tailpipe emissions, you could hardly claim that it’s an unknown quantity – a fact that Tristan understands.

“Our customers have been exploring electrification for some time now,” explains Daniel Barbu, Tristan’s Vice President and General Manager. “With generous incentives from Écocamionnage, as well as the current extremely high cost of fuel, the business case for going electric has never been stronger … we’re looking forward to helping Workhorse expand its customer base across Canada and supporting our customers meet their own goals for sustainability and vehicle performance.”

Tristan operates out of seven locations throughout Québec and Ontario. In addition to its Workhorse offerings, the company is also an authorized Michelin tire, Cummins engine, and Vanguard trailer dealer.

Honda just showed off its first high-end electric mountain bike

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Honda makes just about everything with a motor, from lawnmowers and generators to cars, motorcycles, and even airplanes. Now the Japanese giant is adding another electrified vehicle to the list – a premium electric mountain bike.

Unveiled at EICMA 2026 in Milan, the new Honda RN0 marks the company’s first entry into the premium electric mountain bike market. And unlike many automotive-branded e-bikes that amount to little more than a licensing deal and a familiar logo slapped on someone else’s frame, Honda appears to have actually put some engineering muscle behind this one.

The RN0 was jointly developed with German high-end e-MTB manufacturer ROTWILD and features a proprietary carbon monocoque frame and distinctive gull-arm swingarm inspired by Honda’s motorcycle chassis development experience.

There’s actually some real history behind that connection. Honda experimented with mountain bike development in the early 2000s, culminating in its wild RN01 downhill racing bike. In 2005, that project won the overall series championship in the UCI Mountain Bike Downhill World Cup. Honda never turned the RN01 into a consumer bicycle, but more than two decades later, the RN0 is effectively picking up where the company previously left off.

The roughly 24 kg (53 lb) RN0 is set up as a multi-role electric mountain bike, featuring 160 mm FOX 36 FLOAT Factory Kashima front suspension paired with a FOX FLOAT X Factory Kashima rear shock. DT Swiss H1900 SPLINE wheels use a mullet setup with a 29-inch wheel up front and 27.5-inch wheel in the rear, while Shimano DEORE XT four-piston brakes handle stopping duties.

Those are some quality components, but the drivetrain may be even more interesting.

Honda is using Shimano’s new 2.3 kg drive unit, rated for up to 100 Nm of torque and 750 watts of maximum output, paired with a sizeable 720 Wh battery.

Shimano’s AUTO ASSIST can automatically vary motor assistance based on terrain and rider input, while AUTO SHIFT handles gear changes to maintain an appropriate cadence.

There’s even an Assist Carryover function that briefly continues providing power after pedaling stops, potentially making it easier to clear rocks, roots, and other technical obstacles that normally would see the assist grind to a halt right when it is needed most.

A 2.2-inch color display is integrated directly into the top tube, and there’s USB-C charging plus Shimano app connectivity for adjusting motor settings and electronically locking the bike.

Honda hasn’t yet announced pricing in its release, but with a carbon chassis, Factory-level FOX suspension, XT brakes, and premium Shimano electronics, don’t expect this to be the everyman’s electric bike.

If anything, the RN0 looks much more like Honda is finally bringing its motorcycle engineering mentality back to mountain bikes – this time with a battery and motor along for the ride.

HH Princess Sophia Wolkonsky brings royalty, global business leaders and esports figures together in Vienna for Castillia 2026

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The Castillia founder and CEO hosted an exclusive three-day Heritage Gala and marked the launch of AVOR, with HRH Prince Omar bin Faisal of Jordan, HRH Prince Gabriel of Nassau of Luxembourg, Johann Strobl, Vlad Marinescu and TNW CEO Alexandru Stan among the attendees.

From left: HH Princess Dr. Michaela Wolkonsky, ⁠HIRH Archduchess Herta Margarete Habsburg-Lothringen, HRH Princess Fenosoa Ralandison Ratsimamanga of Madagascar, HH Princess Sophia Wolkonsky.

VIENNA, Austria, HH Princess Sophia Wolkonsky, founder and CEO of Castillia, brought together members of royal families, international business leaders, technology executives and esports figures in Vienna from October 30 to November 1 for Castillia’s Heritage Gala 2026, an invitation-only celebration of cultural heritage, innovation and international connections.

Under her leadership, the three-day gathering connected guests from royalty, finance, entrepreneurship, technology, sports and culture across several of Vienna’s historic venues.

Among the attendees were HRH Prince Omar bin Faisal of Jordan, President of the Jordan Esports Federation; HRH Prince Gabriel of Nassau, a member of Luxembourg’s Grand Ducal family; Johann Strobl, former CEO of Raiffeisen Bank International; Vlad Marinescu, President of the International Esports Federation; and Alexandru Stan, CEO of The Next Web (TNW).

Founded and led by Princess Sophia Wolkonsky, Castillia focuses on historic properties, cultural heritage and curated experiences that connect tradition with contemporary international communities. The Vienna Heritage Gala reflected that vision, pairing formal celebrations with conversations about the future of cultural preservation.

The programme, themed The Enchanted Forest, included an opening reception, a Heritage Salon with panel discussions, a formal gala dinner and a farewell brunch. Its central milestone was the launch of AVOR, a new nonprofit cultural initiative.

Esports was among the emerging industries attracting interest from participants at the Vienna gathering, although it was not the official focus of the gala.

HRH Prince Omar bin Faisal has played an influential role in esports through his leadership in Jordan and involvement with international esports organizations. Vlad Marinescu, President of the International Esports Federation, also attended, bringing an international perspective on competitive gaming and its institutional development.

Filip Šoć, President of the Alliance of Electronic Sports of Montenegro and founder of Montenegro Future Festival, was also present. HRH Prince Gabriel of Nassau also expressed interest in the sector.

The gathering also included Johann Strobl, former CEO of Raiffeisen Bank International, alongside entrepreneurs and leaders from other sectors. Their presence illustrated the event’s breadth beyond traditional cultural and royal circles.

The inauguration of AVOR was a central moment of Castillia 2026. According to the official invitation, the international nonprofit initiative is developing a Global Cultural Atlas, with young people documenting the stories and places that shape cultural heritage.

The launch took place on October 30 at Vienna Private Members, Wiener Rennverein, at Palais Esterházy. On October 31, the Heritage Salon was held at Park Hyatt Vienna, followed by the formal Heritage Gala at Palais Pallavicini. The programme concluded on November 1.

The event was held under the patronage of figures including HRH Prince Omar bin Faisal of Jordan, Princess Ratsimamanga Ralandison Fenosoa, Maharani Sahiba Nivritti Kumari Mewar of Udaipur, Duke Michael zu Mecklenburg and Prince Constantin Sturdza.

By bringing historic families, cultural leaders, business executives and figures from emerging industries into the same setting, Princess Sophia Wolkonsky’s Castillia offered a meeting point between the preservation of the past and the opportunities of the future.

Disclosure: TNW CEO Alexandru Stan attended Castillia 2026 as a guest. TNW received no payment for this article, and Castillia did not cover travel or accommodation for our attendance.

Black Nails: The Little Black Manicure Is Having a Major Moment

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

A slick of jet-black nail polish never goes out of style, and is equal to more than the sum of its parts. It can read grungy, or glam, as a surprisingly versatile shade; and now the inky manicure is enjoying a run as fall’s chicest nail trend. Forget elaborate nail art, milky neutrals, or even the season’s customary burgundy and Bordeaux, because Hailey Bieber, Dua Lipa, Kylie Jenner, and Selena Gomez have all been going back to black.

At the 2026 Academy Museum Gala, Bieber offered a particularly polished take on the trend, pairing her bubblegum-pink Chanel gown with glossy, jet-black nails. Her go-to nail artist, Zola Ganzorigt, used OPI Intelli-Gel in Black Onyx, shaping the nails into elongated almonds and finishing them with a lacquer-like shine. The jewel-black shade was a striking counterpoint to the sweetness of her dress—a glam goth moment—and a marked departure from the glazed, barely-there manicures that have become a Mrs. Hailey Bieber signature.

For others, black nails aren’t just a seasonal trend, but a regular fixture in their nail appointment calendar. Dua Lipa has long favored a midnight manicure, wearing the shade even in the height of summer, when brighter colors and fun decals typically reign supreme. Kylie Jenner, too, is a longtime devotee. At a Kylie Cosmetics holiday party, she paired glossy black almond-shaped nails with a frothy, feather-trimmed pink dress. Rather than adding a pop of color to a neutral outfit, both Jenner and Bieber made the case for doing the inverse, grounding a sugary look with something dark and decidedly cool.

Hailey Bieber’s glam goth manicure at the 2026 Academy Museum Gala.

There are plenty of ways to play with the formula, too. Selena Gomez has previously experimented with a black velvet manicure, courtesy of her longtime nail artist Tom Bachik, lending the otherwise simple shade a soft, light-catching dimension. Bachik used Chanel’s Le Vernis in Le Diable En Chanel; after meticulously shaping and prepping the nails, he applied the house’s La Base Camélia before layering on the deep, almost-black lacquer.

Selena Gomez’s “velvet noir” manicure, courtesy of Tom Bachik.

For the festive season, a dusting of chrome or a magnetic cat-eye finish can bring a little extra drama. Shape makes a difference, too: longer almond nails, as favored by Bieber and Jenner, feel sleek and glamorous, while shorter, squarer silhouettes offer a more understated, slightly punky alternative.

Part of black polish’s enduring appeal is that it works across skin tones, nail lengths, and personal styles—nail artist Julia Diogo even describes them as “winter nude.” It’s equally at home with a slouchy knit and jeans as it is with an outre dress, and has enough presence to make even the simplest outfit feel considered. And while there’s certainly room for embellishment, there’s something quite chic about keeping things so simple. No nail art, no glitter (yet!): just two coats of glossy noir.

Denver Airport Taco Bell Worker Sues Over Unpaid Security Time. So Much For My $1.70 Burritos?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

I had barely finished celebrating $1.70 burritos at Denver Airport when View From The Wing highlighted a lawsuit that might help explain why airport food usually costs so much more. No, we did not coordinate our Taco Bell coverage. Apparently, we just had different concerns about what happens before someone hands me a burrito.

A former Taco Bell food preparer at Denver International Airport is suing the restaurant’s operator over allegedly unpaid time spent passing through security and traveling to the restaurant before clocking in. The Colorado state court lawsuit also alleges failures to provide paid rest breaks, according to Law360.

Meanwhile, I had just written about finding surprisingly affordable Taco Bell food in Concourse A. A reader noticed the coincidence and wondered about the relationship between my cheap burritos and Gary’s discussion of employee compensation.

My first instinct is to distinguish getting to work from doing the job. Most employees do not get paid for their commute, even when traffic makes it miserable. An airport restaurant operator does not control the security checkpoint, either.

But Colorado’s wage rules give the worker a substantial argument. They specifically include security or safety screening, and waiting for it, among activities that can constitute compensable time worked. The rules also provide circumstances in which travel between work locations must be paid once work has begun.

That raises a question beyond the checkpoint itself: if required screening starts the paid workday, does the subsequent journey to the restaurant count too?

At Denver Airport, getting through security does not necessarily mean you are anywhere near your workstation.

Taco Bell can reasonably argue that the screening and journey remain part of the commute and the lawsuit, on its own, does not establish that wages were unlawfully withheld. Still, Colorado’s express inclusion of security screening makes this a claim worth taking seriously.

I often roll my eyes at airport food prices. My recent Denver visit included a nearly $20 barbecue salad and a $6.25 street taco that left me much less satisfied than Taco Bell.

But this dispute illustrates a genuine expense associated with operating inside an airport.

If employees must be paid while waiting for screening and making their way to the restaurant, the operator is paying for time before anyone starts preparing food or serving customers. That expense has to be absorbed somewhere. Some of it could end up in menu prices. Indeed, it must.

Every overpriced airport meal is not suddenly justified, but workers should not forfeit compensation required by law so I can enjoy a cheap lunch. It is a more convincing explanation for a price premium than simply charging more because passengers have nowhere else to go…

I hope those $1.70 burritos stick around. After praising them, I would prefer not to discover that I had written their farewell tribute.

But if airport employees must be paid for additional time getting through security and reaching their workplace, higher operating costs will put pressure on those bargains. I can appreciate affordable food and recognize that providing it inside an airport comes with expenses the neighborhood Taco Bell does not face. Gary and I managed to cover both sides of that equation without even comparing notes!

Matthew is an avid traveler who calls Los Angeles home. Each year he travels more than 200,000 miles by air and has visited more than 135 countries. Working both in the aviation industry and as a travel consultant, Matthew has been featured in major media outlets around the world and uses his Live and Let's Fly blog to share the latest news in the airline industry, commentary on frequent flyer programs, and detailed reports of his worldwide travel.

Personally I just enjoyed yesterday’s Denver dichotomy, but I suppose Part 3 of the Taco Bell Trilogy will need to include a discussion of the economics of a loss leader item like the rice and beans burrito. Presumably there is some interest in continuing to keep that item at a lower cost to help drive traffic – whether that $1.70 goes up to $1.99 who knows (only “the market” I suppose), but probably won’t go to $2.50. As with any classic loss leader item, it often leads to the purchase of a higher value item in tandem – in your case, a still relatively modest $3 steak burrito.

Of course, you ended up adding steak to the $1.70 rice and beans burrito making it cost $3.50 instead of $1.70. Was that worth it versus the $3 steak burrito? Inquiring minds and all.

You are a careful reader. I like the bean/rice burrito because it comes with jalapeño sauce, which otherwise would cost 70 cents to add since Taco Bell doesn’t let you “swap” sauces on almost every item. But both items were fine.

But Wait! I thought if we increased the operating costs to run a business, the costs to the consumer WON’T go up!

Because that’s what 1990 always says when he wants to implement EU261 in the US, or wants higher compensation for delays, or wants delays out of the control of the airlines to be considered “within their control”, or wants airlines to increase reserve aircraft and crew coverage, etc. etc. etc. He says that can be done without increasing air fares.

Oh well. Cue to “anti-worker” or “anti-consumer” or “pro-fat-cat-corporation” label to be thrown my way.

Yes, sometimes those consumer protections create a far more smoother, predictable, and thereby efficient system and those who “pay a little more” actually don’t lose, because they are protected too.

Sort of like we don’t need “free riders” who claim they don’t need health insurance because of “muh freedom” but then clog ER waiting rooms when something goes bad because they know that they will not be turned away.

Book Review: Forward Deployed: Inside the Palantir Framework That Solved Enterprise AI by Riste Gramatikov

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Ever since The Lean Startup by Eric Ries was published in 2011, prevailing wisdom in the SaaS community has been to launch a minimum viable product (MVP) rather than wait for a perfect, feature-complete release. The lesson was to release quickly, gather real user feedback, and continuously improve through small, iterative updates instead of relying on assumptions.

Another foundational SaaS principle is “Validate Before You Scale.” The assumption is that you shouldn’t invest heavily in building a complete software product before proving that customers actually want it. Launch the MVP, validate demand with real users, and expand functionality based on demonstrated needs.

Another principle closely associated with Paul Graham and the early philosophy of Y Combinator is to build something a small number of people love rather than something a large number of people merely like. In theory, this allows a product to scale as more users discover that they cannot operate without the software being offered.

The thing is, Palantir challenges the conventional way these rules are applied. The book Forward Deployed: Inside the Palantir Framework That Solved Enterprise AI by Riste Gramatikov reveals how this is done. It is less about abandoning customer feedback than taking it much further, embedding engineers inside the businesses whose problems they are trying to solve.

Palantir approaches its business through first-principles thinking. This extends from its internal workings to its Forward Deployed Engineer (FDE) model, which embeds software engineers directly with customers to understand their problems firsthand, develop tailored solutions, and continuously refine the software based on real-world usage.

Rather than separating developers from end users, Palantir creates a direct feedback loop between engineering and customers. This allows the company to solve immediate problems while incorporating lessons learned into its core platform, making the software increasingly valuable and scalable across its customer base.

Traditional SaaS principles would suggest that this could not scale properly, as each business has unique requirements. While it is true that this is not a one-size-fits-all approach, it also enabled Palantir to build a moat. The difficult work is connecting software to the realities of supply chains, logistics, manufacturing, inventory management, and the often highly specific requirements of government entities and Fortune 500 companies.

What initially looks like a scaling problem can become a competitive advantage if the lessons from one deployment improve the next. The distinction is whether a company keeps rebuilding bespoke software from scratch or uses customer-specific work to strengthen a shared platform.

What sets this book apart from regular business books is its composite simulation based on real Palantir implementations, with a level of operational detail that is rarely accessible in a single account. The book is an independent analysis drawn from public sources, and the simulated company gives those ideas a concrete setting.

I was enthralled by the case study of MidWest Manufacturing. I was also impressed with the explanations behind the decision-making and how those decisions benefited both Palantir and its customers. Following the same business through the different stages makes it easier to understand how individual choices accumulate into a larger operating model.

Peter Thiel has been strongly influenced by the philosopher René Girard, whose theory describes human desire as fundamentally imitative. Businesses also fall into this trap. For the most part, they converge on the same strategies, enter the same markets, and compete with similar products.

The book’s account of Palantir offers a way to think about escaping this mimetic behavior. Three features of that approach stood out to me:

I often found myself taking notes on passages in the book. Many offered a contrarian view that, when inspected through the lens of Palantir, made complete sense.

For example, the book closes by framing the choice facing enterprises in stark terms: whether they will be among the 5% that succeed in transforming or the 95% that fall behind. I read this as a challenge to business leaders to turn AI investment into something that changes how their organizations operate.

And this is precisely the reality businesses find themselves in. If everyone is doing the same thing as everyone else, there is no differentiation and no moat. The company becomes another competing product offering much the same thing. This book offers a rare instructional guide to how Palantir escaped that trap, focusing heavily on forward deployed engineers and avoiding mimetic behavior.

All of this feeds into Palantir’s compound learning flywheel. The principle is that every customer deployment should make the underlying software platform smarter, more capable, and more valuable for future customers.

Through its forward deployed engineers, Palantir identifies real-world problems, develops solutions, and feeds those insights back into its core software. Instead of repeatedly building custom solutions from scratch, the company turns recurring challenges into reusable capabilities that benefit subsequent deployments.

This creates a compounding feedback loop: more customers generate more insights, more insights produce better software, and better software enables faster, more effective deployments. Over time, the accumulated knowledge becomes a competitive advantage that is increasingly difficult for competitors to replicate.

I’ve read hundreds of books on AI and business models, and they often converge on the same strategies and repeat the same insights. This book manages to contribute something new. It offered a rare glimpse into the workings of one of the most unique and contrarian companies in Silicon Valley.

I recommend Forward Deployed to anyone launching a business that touches SaaS, supply chains, logistics, manufacturing, or enterprise AI. For me, its value lies in showing how the difficult work of solving a customer’s specific problem can become the foundation of a business that keeps getting stronger with every deployment.

A serial entrepreneur, he believes that AI will be as disruptive to society as electricity, and is often caught raving about the potential of disruptive technologies and AGI.

As a futurist, he is dedicated to exploring how these innovations will shape our world. In addition, he is the founder of Securities.io, a platform focused on investing in cutting-edge technologies that are redefining the future and reshaping entire sectors.

This Clever Chopstick Trick Shows When Houseplants Need Water – No More Guessing or Soggy Soil

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Sign up to get fresh gardening ideas, inspiration and expert advice delivered straight to your inbox.

7 Household Trash Items You Should Save in Fall to Protect Plants From Frost

7 Foolproof Plants Anyone Can Propagate From Cuttings in November – Grow More of Your Favorite Shrubs for Free

Got Fungus Gnats? This Easy Potato Trick Lures Hidden Larvae Out of Houseplant Soil

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Dry potting mix doesn't always mean a thirsty plant. A chopstick inserted near the roots tells you everything – and you may already have one in a kitchen drawer.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Figuring out when to water houseplants seems like an easy call, right up until you're standing over a pot with the watering can. The top of the mix looks pale and dusty. You stick a finger in; it's dry, so you water. Then, a week or two later, the lower leaves go yellow, and it turns out the soil underneath was damp the whole time.

Too much water is behind a lot of the trouble people run into when watering houseplants, and fall makes it easier to get it wrong. Days are shorter now, and the light is weaker, so growth slows down, and a pot can stay wet far longer than it did back in July. Roots sitting in soggy mix can't get the air they need – and that's how rot gets started.

Your finger only reaches the first inch or so. In a deeper pot, that top layer can dry out days before the root zone does. One fix is a moisture meter, like the Yamron 4-in-1 from Amazon. They're not expensive and offer an accurate assessment of soil moisture. But another way of checking is to use a wooden chopstick – it costs nothing, if there's one left over from takeout in a drawer somewhere, and it reaches right down to where the roots are.

Bare wood soaks up moisture. Push an untreated chopstick or bamboo skewer down into damp potting mix, and the wood darkens wherever it touches wet soil, and crumbs of mix stick to it when you pull it out. Dry mix behaves differently. Dry particles just fall away, and the stick comes back pale and mostly clean.

Have you ever done the toothpick test on a cake? It's the same idea, just backward. With a cake you want the toothpick clean, but in a pot, a clean chopstick means the mix has dried out at that depth.

One important point: you must use plain wood for this. Lacquered or painted chopsticks shed water, so they won't darken much. A plastic one only shows you whatever soil happens to cling to it, and not much else, honestly.

Grab a clean, dry stick first. Any leftover dampness from the last check will throw off the reading. Standard chopsticks are long enough for small and medium pots, but big floor plants sit in deeper containers, and that's where 12-inch bamboo skewers come in handy. Walmart sells a pack of 100 for a few dollars.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Push the stick in partway between the main stem and the rim, straight down into the root zone. Did it hit resistance on the way? Ease off and try another spot, because forcing it through a thick mass of roots can damage them. Then leave it there, alone, for about 30 seconds to a minute so the wood has time to pick up any moisture.

Slide it out slowly and look along the whole length. If there's a dark band near the bottom with mix stuck to it, the lower part of the pot is still wet – even if the top inch came out clean.

If you still can't make sense of the stick, use a soil moisture meter or a leave-in option, like the Sustee Aquameter, which sits in the pot full-time and fades from blue to white as the mix dries. Either way, the reading only tells you what's in the pot. What you do next depends on the plant.

A damp chopstick generally means there's still water down there, and a clean, dry one suggests the mix has dried out at the depth you tested. That isn't an automatic cue to grab the watering can, though. Peace lilies need their mix kept moist but never soggy, so water once the stick starts coming out dry through the upper part of the pot. Wait much longer, and the whole plant usually droops hard.

Pothos can go drier than that, a fair bit drier. Let the stick come out dry through a good share of the pot before you water. Snake plants and succulents go further still, and they're safest when the stick comes back clean all the way to the bottom. In winter, a snake plant may only need water every month or two. When one of them has been sitting in soggy mix for weeks, repot it into a fast-draining mix, like Miracle-Gro cactus, palm and citrus mix from Home Depot.

Chunky orchid bark is the big one. Those chunks are too coarse to stick to wood, and the bark soaks up water inside each piece rather than holding it on the surface. You can pull a clean, dry-looking chopstick out of a pot that's still plenty moist. Gritty, very coarse mixes muddle things the same way. A badly rootbound plant is tricky too, just in a different way. There's hardly any mix left between the roots, the stick won't go in easily, and water poured on top can run straight down the sides and out the drainage holes.

Use the chopstick as one clue, then. It can't measure anything precisely, so back it up with a few other checks. Pick the pot up right after a good soak and again a few days later, and you'll learn how light it feels once it's dry. Check the leaves as well. Yellowing lower leaves on a heavy pot usually point to too much water. And make sure every pot drains freely. Clear nursery pots with drainage holes, like these from Walmart, let you see roots and dark, wet mix through the side.

A calendar can't see inside a pot. If you water on the same day every week, it can go fine all summer and then start leaving roots wet for days once fall arrives and the light drops off.

Chopsticks are about as cheap as plant tools get, and one shows what's happening under the surface while watering needs shift with the seasons. Keep a dry one next to the watering can. It'll be right there the next time the soil has you guessing.

Tyler’s passion began with indoor gardening and deepened as he studied plant-fungi interactions in controlled settings. With a microbiology background focused on fungi, he’s spent over a decade solving tough and intricate gardening problems. After spinal injuries and brain surgery, Tyler’s approach to gardening changed. It became less about the hobby and more about recovery and adapting to physical limits. His growing success shows that disability doesn’t have to stop you from your goals.

SWIFT Debuts Own XRP-Inclusive Ledger With Dozen Banks

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Home » Ripple (XRP) » SWIFT Debuts Own XRP-Inclusive Ledger With Dozen Banks

Ripple’s XRP extends connections to multiple banks in SWIFT’s blockchain-adopting line-up.

The biggest European financial conglomerate’s grandiose move to release their own blockchain ledger didn’t go unnoticed. This November as planned, the Society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunication (SWIFT) debuted the pilot version with 12 banks, some of which are familiar names in the XRP holder community.

One of them, HSBC, is slated to start blockchain-powered operations. HSBC connects its own Tokenised Deposit Service (TDS) to SWIFT’s ledger. The major British bank actively employs Metaco’s Harmonize platform for institutional digital-asset custody (Metaco was acquired by Ripple in 2023). The enterprise-level acquisition strengthened that bond.

5 OF SWIFT'S 17 LEDGER BANKS ALREADY WORK WITH RIPPLE 🚨Swift's new blockchain ledger is live with 17 banks piloting 24/7 cross-border payments.Look who's on that list:🏦 HSBC🏦 Standard Chartered🏦 DBS🏦 FirstRand🏦 BNY, which holds RLUSD's reserves and now holds XRP… https://t.co/z7UOp1KBYz pic.twitter.com/nNP5Ql4vgn

The bank also has historical references to XRPL capabilities in internal materials and some past explorations, with an agenda to expand blockchain-powered business operations. Conducting business operations on Distributed Ledger Technology (DLT) based chains like Ripple’s XRP Ledger serves an advantage of instant settlement & cost-effectiveness.

Aside from them, there’s a few more in the 12-long list. Standard Chartered, another popular British bank, has been running the XRP-related tech stack since 2016. During October 2026 the traditional finance behemoth launched their institutional custody for Ripple’s RLUSD stablecoin on the XRP Ledger through their Dubai (DIFC) branch.

Also, there’s DBS and BNY (BNY Mellon). These two Ripple-tied major banks serve completely different purposes. BNY Mellon is the primary reserve custodian for RLUSD. Meanwhile, DBS is partnered with Franklin Templeton to list a tokenized money-market fund (sgBENJI) on XRP Ledger, tradeable against RLUSD on their digital exchange.

🚨 ES OFICIALSWIFT acaba de confirmar transacciones en vivo en blockchain con más de 12 bancos.Y los reportes dicen que está impulsado por $XRP.Este es el tipo de noticia que la gente lleva años esperando.El sistema antiguo finalmente está siendo reemplazado en tiempo real?… pic.twitter.com/2uJi0VGPI9

This is a distinct product from the SWIFT project. SWIFT’s blockchain ledger is compatible with multiple DLT networks, but Ripple’s XRP Ledger has a crystal-clear advantage in regulatory compliance and native traits. XRP, along with Stellar’s XLM & Hedera’s HBAR has native ISO 20022 support, the new gold standard in financial messaging.

The cross-border payments market, worth over $155 trillion in processed volume annually, powers global trade, remittances & commerce. Yet merchants admit it remains slow, costly and not too efficient when compared to blockchain-based counterparts. 70% of merchants expect all transactions to settle immediately by 2027, which aligns with SWIFT’s vision.

SWIFT just named 30+ @Ripple connected banks in its new 2026 payments framework.HSBC, Deutsche Bank, Santander, Standard Chartered, JPMorgan… the list goes on.XRP isn’t replacing SWIFT. It’s becoming the liquidity bridge inside it. https://t.co/Y59ziIog9J

How much of that multi-trillion pie Ripple’s XRP can take is yet to be determined. On the SWIFT topic, Ripple’s CEO Brad Garlinghouse predicted a 14% capture. However, Ripple’s business ventures don’t always translate into blockchain adoption, as seen in the 2025 example. Ripple Prime, the custody arm, processed over $13 trillion without the help of crypto.

Arsenal transfer interest faces major obstacle as Santos secure £83m teenage talent

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Arsenal may have to rethink their interest in Santos forward Ignacio Pereira after the Brazilian club secured the teenager’s future with a substantial release clause.

The 16-year-old has caught the eye of a number of European clubs after an impressive campaign in Santos’ youth teams. The likes of Arsenal are said to have been watching his progress.

However, Santos have acted swiftly to secure the services of one of their most exciting young talents. Pereira signed his first professional contract with the club on November 4, tying him to the club until December 2029.

The deal also includes a 100 million euro release clause (around £83.4 million), according to Brazilian journalist Bruno Lima from UOL.

This poses a major problem for Arsenal if they are looking to sign the Argentine but also offers a lot of security for Santos, as they want to develop him as a first-team player.

Pereira has signed a new deal amid increasing interest in his development. Reports in Brazil have previously said that Arsenal, Real Betis, AC Milan, Lille, Standard Liège and Zenit are all keeping an eye on the rising star.

The club have now secured his services for another three years. The new agreement was awaiting registration with the Brazilian Football Confederation when the details emerged.

Pereira has spent three years at Santos, having joined from Juventude in 2023. He previously developed at Grêmio before moving to the São Paulo club.

Pereira has certainly lived up to the expectations. He scored an impressive 6 goals in 17 games in the Brazilian Under-17 Championship, making him the second-highest scorer for Santos. He also scored 4 goals in the Paulista Under-17 Championship.

This is very promising. It is his first season playing at under-17 level.

Pereira has been compared to Julián Álvarez due to his mobility and ability to play outside the box.

But the youngster cites another big name up front as his main inspiration. According to UOL, he cites Manchester City’s Erling Haaland as his inspiration, while Lionel Messi is still his football idol.

His profile suggests a versatile number nine who can play lower down the pitch, be an attacking midfielder and play with another striker. He has impressed the youth coaches at Santos with his finishing ability, physicality and desire to win.

He has a long way to go. Reports from Brazil suggest that Santos want to improve his tactical awareness and behaviour before making him more responsible.

He is being considered by the club for inclusion in their squad for the 2027 Copa São Paulo de Futebol Júnior (known as Copinha) and could start on the bench.

This interest from Arsenal does not necessarily mean that there is an imminent move for Pereira. The player is only 16, and a potential deal is made much more complicated due to a new contract.

Maybe the best course of action for Mikel Arteta’s recruitment team is to keep an eye on him. Santos has made it clear where they stand, and Pereira can now work towards his dream. Being in their first team.

‘Ferritin Face’: What Low Iron Actually Does (and Doesn't Do) to Your Appearance

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Social media influencers claim that having a “ferritin face” may indicate you’re low in iron.

Ferritin is a protein that helps your body store iron. You need iron in order to make healthy red blood cells, which carry oxygen from your lungs to the rest of your body. Iron is also important for bone marrow, organs, healthy muscles, and brain development.

As “ferritin face” makes its way across social media platforms, users are describing several tell-tale signs, including:

Users have also shared images of their face before and after taking iron supplements or getting an iron infusion. Videos about “ferritin face” have garnered millions of views on various platforms.

However, medical professionals state that your appearance alone is not enough to diagnose iron deficiency.

“It’s important to understand that ferritin face is not a medical diagnosis, nor is it a confirmed symptom of iron deficiency. While some people with iron deficiency can appear pale due to low hemoglobin levels in their red blood cells, there are many other symptoms to consider,” said Daniel Atkinson, a general practitioner in the United Kingdom and clinical lead at Treated.

“Notably, these symptoms are also present in many different conditions, and their presence does not necessarily mean iron deficiency anemia or even low ferritin levels. Similar features are seen in people with iron processing and absorption issues, such as gluten intolerance, or in urgent sudden blood loss scenarios,” added Jonathan Jennings, MD, board certified internist at Medical Offices of Manhattan.

Iron deficiency can affect your skin and appearance. These signs are often subtle, but may include:

In some ways, the social media videos are correct. Your skin and face can change when your iron levels are low.

“The facial signs people point to, like pale skin and a washed-out look, mostly come from anaemia, when your haemoglobin has dropped. But iron deficiency and anaemia aren’t the same thing,” said Thom Phillips, a general practitioner in the United Kingdom and Chief Medical Officer at Forth. “By the time low iron shows on your face, your stores are usually close to empty.”

It is important to speak with a healthcare professional, who will run blood tests such as a complete blood count and ferritin levels.

It is also important not to take iron supplements without first checking with your healthcare professional, because having too much iron in your body can also cause negative effects.

Too little and too much iron could raise your risk of infection, which is why it’s important not to take supplements without medical supervision.

The signs and symptoms of iron deficiency can depend on whether you are anemic or not. Some of the early signs may include:

“These symptoms typically occur because your body produces fewer red blood cells, which results in less oxygen being delivered throughout your body, making you feel tired and weak,” said Atkinson.

While your facial appearance can be a sign that something isn’t right, healthcare professionals consider other symptoms and how you feel overall.

They also rely on blood tests to confirm the diagnosis and guide treatment.

“The misinformation is the idea that you can spot it yourself. That leads to two problems: people with low iron who look fine assume they’re OK, and people who look tired start taking iron supplements they don’t need,” said Phillips.

Atkinson also noted that factors such as different settings and environmental conditions can affect how your face appears.

That being said, the “ferritin face” trend may help bring awareness to certain symptoms and when to get them checked.

“[This] trend may raise some awareness about iron deficiency anemia and how low iron levels can impact daily life,” said Atkinson.

“Fatigue and lightheadedness are common side effects of low iron, but it manifests in other ways too, such as paler skin or increased hair shedding, which can be concerning for many people. If you experience these symptoms, you should let your doctor know so they can help treat the condition appropriately,” he said.

This Totally Free App Can Free Up Space on Your Mac

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Your Mac's hard drive is full ... again. What's taking up all that space? Endless applications promise to answer this question and help you free up space, most with a subscription. I've tested many over the years, and few are easier to recommend than Purge.

This totally free, open source app gives you a quick breakdown of what's taking up space on your hard drive and offers a few specific ways to free it up. Even better: There are no ads or upsells. Purge can even delete Apple Intelligence's offline models from macOS 27, which could free up over 10 GB.

But even if you want to keep Apple's AI features, you can still clear up plenty of space. Your Mac, like any computer, tends to build up clutter over time. Sure, you might have forgotten about the games, images, and videos you downloaded, but caches also take up space. These are files downloaded and saved to speed things up, which can be useful. The problem is they can stick around longer than necessary and take up space. And then there are caches and settings left behind by apps you uninstalled ages ago—they're still there, eating up precious bytes.

Purge is designed to help you clean all that out, but it can do more. It's built around lists of files known to be safe to purge. There's also a list of things it will never touch, including crucial system files and your own documents. Finally, Purge sends files to the Trash instead of deleting them outright, so you can always recover them if something breaks.

To get started, download Purge and install it. Open the application, and it will show you what's taking up space on your Mac.

If you want, click the Clean Safe Items button, and the app will handle it, assuming you trust it. I recommend paying a bit more attention, though. First, delete some caches. You'll see a list of caches, all of which are safe to purge periodically.

You'll also see Apple's Aerial screen savers here. These videos are very cool, but take up a lot of space—delete them if you, like most 21st-century people, don't use a screen saver.

There's also a Dev Tools section, which isn't relevant for most people. If you use Homebrew, though, don't skip this—it's an easy way to delete cached installation files you don't need anymore.

The next tool shows you only the largest files on your computer, likely videos or other large media. I ended up moving a few things I'd forgotten about off my laptop—I bet you'll find something, too.

Up next is a tool for uninstalling apps. Note that this doesn't just remove the application—it also removes every file associated with it, including your settings, caches, and anything the application downloaded. Deleting applications in Finder doesn't do this, meaning you might leave all kinds of files behind.

Speaking of that, there's also a tool that shows leftover data from applications you've already deleted. In my case, it was several gigabytes, all basically wasted. I highly recommend checking this section and deleting anything large that you're confident you won't use again.

Finally, so long as we're talking about things you're not using, there's Apple Intelligence. Apple's most recent operating systems come preloaded with offline AI models, which power things like notification summaries, writing suggestions, and the new AI-powered Siri. You might like and use these features, but if not, Apple removed the toggle in macOS 27 that lets you disable them altogether.

That's why Purge has one final tool that can completely remove the Apple Intelligence models. Click it, and you'll see a list of installed models above a delete button. Click the button, and Purge will delete the models and prevent macOS from reinstalling them.

These features are why I plan on keeping Purge around. It's a great way to see what's taking up space on your drive and then do something about it.

US sanctions Canadian money-exchange firms over Iran ties

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

US also offers as much as $20 million and possible relocation for information about Iran’s nuclear program.

The US Treasury imposed financial sanctions on eight Canadian money-service companies it claims have done business with sanctioned ‌Iranian entities.

These companies facilitated the exchange of Iranian rials and other currencies for digital assets, enabling international transactions, a US Treasury Department statement said on Friday.

The sanctions, which serve to isolate the companies from the dollar-based financial system, are the latest in a series of Treasury moves against firms outside the US.

These businesses have facilitated transactions with sanctioned Iranian digital asset exchanges, as well as with digital asset addresses associated with Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), according to the Treasury.

Related: US plans to seize $1B in crypto linked to Iran this week: Scott Bessent

“Canada cannot be a safe haven for illicit financial facilitators that provide sanctions evasion services to the Iranian regime and its proxies,” said Secretary of the Treasury Scott Bessent. “Treasury will pursue and disrupt the Iranian regime’s financial channels anywhere in the world.”

Cointelegraph reported in August that the Treasury Department’s Office of Foreign Assets Control, or OFAC, was targeting crypto exchanges facilitating the transfer of funds to Iran’s IRGC. Bessent said at the time that the US planned to “increase the economic pressure” on Iran, focusing on financial networks funding the country’s regime “in dollars, rials, or crypto.”

Friday’s designations are a continuation of Treasury’s campaign to “disrupt the regime’s exploitation of digital assets to fund terrorism, human rights abuses, and attacks on US personnel and allies,” the statement said.

It added that the actions were coordinated with FINTRAC, Canada’s financial intelligence unit.

Some of the entities subject to this action have changed their names and addresses to evade detection, the statement noted.

In a separate action on Friday, the US Rewards for Justice program offered as much as $20 million and possible relocation for information about Iran’s nuclear program, including its facilities, equipment and personnel.

Iran’s lack of compliance with the International Atomic Energy Agency on safeguards has led to the agency’s loss of “continuity of knowledge” regarding Iran’s production of enriched uranium and components like centrifuges, the statement said.

US charges three men over $129m scheme that faked CEOs’ voices with AI

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Prosecutors in New York say the men posed as executives and lawyers on calls, then laundered the money through crypto.

The men are Jacob Soall, 41, Sharon Hazan, 54, and Guy Sheetrit, 42. Spanish police arrested Soall and Hazan in June and August, and UK police arrested Sheetrit in September. The US has asked for all three to be extradited.

The scheme ran from 2024 to 2026, according to the indictment. The men would call an employee while posing as the company’s chief executive, using AI to mimic the boss’s voice or image. They would ask for help with a secret deal.

The fake CEO then told the employee to contact a lawyer at a large international law firm. Another member of the group posed as that lawyer and gave the bank details for a large transfer.

The victims included a Missouri hospital system, which lost about $4.8m, and a UK fintech company, which lost about $11.6m. A Rhode Island bank, a California hospital system, a Massachusetts mutual fund, and a Florida pet food company were also hit.

The money went to accounts in countries including Spain, Greece, the UAE, Singapore, and China. It was then turned into cryptocurrency to hide where it came from, prosecutors said.

Soall mainly posed as UK lawyers and kept a handwritten script called Lawyer Call, according to the indictment. Hazan and Sheetrit are accused of laundering the money. Hazan was convicted of bank fraud conspiracy in the same court in 2008.

The three face charges including wire fraud, money laundering, and aggravated identity theft. The most serious counts carry up to 20 years in prison. The charges are allegations, and the men are presumed innocent.

The case is a larger version of a 2019 voice-cloning fraud, in which criminals faked a CEO’s voice to steal $243,000. Since then, AI impersonation scams have grown fast.
