Here’s Why You Shouldn’t Be Afraid to Harvest Does During the Rut

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 7, 2026 10:00 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

If there’s anything deer hunters can agree on, it’s that we love to argue. There’s plenty to debate: scent control vs. hunting the wind, treestand vs. saddle hunting, compounds vs. crossbows — the list goes on. Unsurprisingly, the advisability of harvesting does during the rut is no different.

Like many hunters this season, I’ve been patiently waiting for any mature deer to slip up during the craziness of the rut. Because, alongside the lone Pennsylvania buck tag in my pocket, are plenty of doe tags. A couple days ago, after passing a few doe fawns and young bucks in hopes that a more mature doe or buck walked through, I began to wonder: How many hunters are purposefully passing does during the rut? And more importantly, is it a good idea to wait or shoot? That’s why I reached out to Lindsay Thomas Jr., chief communications officer of the National Deer Association, for advice.

There are two lines of reasoning on why hunters should or should not shoot does during the rut. The more does running around, one argument goes, the more bucks those does will attract. That camp passes does during the rut because a live doe might walk by and bring a big buck in on her trail, or to the area you’re hunting when she goes into estrus in a few days.

I fall into the other camp. I agree that nothing beats a real doe’s scent — which is why I want to put a doe down nearby. Thomas agrees.

“My personal belief is there’s no better attractant for bucks during the rut than a doe on the ground in your hunting area,” says Thomas. “There is no better attractant. I don’t care what’s in a bottle. I don’t care what calls you’re using, nothing beats it.”

Both Thomas and I have experienced just how successful dropping a doe near your stand during the rut can be. Last year I dropped a doe about 40 yards from my stand early into an afternoon rut hunt. About an hour later, two small bucks came in to check her out, both presenting broadside shots multiple times. I chose to pass those two, but had they been bigger, more mature bucks, one wouldn’t have been so lucky.

“Many other hunters I know have seen this strategy work,” says Thomas. “Dr. Grant Woods has got some great video that he captured showing a buck actually using its antlers trying to get the dead doe to stand back up. By understanding tending behavior, we know that when a buck separates an estrus doe from the other deer, she will typically bed down until she’s ready to breed. Oftentimes this involves the bedded doe laying for upwards of an hour or two just chewing her cud until she’s ready. So, a doe lying on the ground smelling like an estrus doe and looking like an estrus doe is not at all alarming to a buck this time of year. In fact, it’s totally natural.”

If you’re interested in trying to fill an antlerless tag and shoot a buck during the same hunt, it’s important to do your best to put that doe down close to your setup. It’s not unusual for deer to run up to 100 yards after the shot (particularly if you’re bowhunting). In my mind, if a doe piles up that far away, it lowers the likelihood that a buck comes into bow range if he cruises by to check her out.

True, it may not matter because a buck could follow her scent on the exact same trail she took and present the same great shot. It’s also just as likely that another buck cruising through the area catches a whiff of her where she fell and beelines straight to her. If she’s lying dead out of sight, you may never see him. Personally, I get down, tag her, and bring her closer to my setup as quickly and quietly as possible. (Plus, in Pennsylvania, you have to tag the first deer you harvested before filling any other tags.)

Thomas is less inclined to climb out of his stand.

“I don’t think you want to get down and spend an hour blood trailing if it’s not going to be an easy blood trail,” says Thomas. “Walking all around that area is a ‘no’ for me, but if you saw her go down in some brush and know right where she is and you can make it quick, dragging her into the open where she’s visual from more areas might be a good thing for the remainder of your hunt.”

It’s worth noting that it is a hunter’s duty to always ethically dispatch and recover the first animal you shot, so make sure to pick your shots wisely, make smart choices about the recovery, and, if you’re choosing to use the doe as an attractant, only do so on cold days where the meat won’t spoil while you sit for a few more hours.

Regardless of your opinions on rut tactics, the NDA has been a proponent of harvesting does as early in the season as possible to meet your properties management goals. Experts say shooting whitetail does during the rut can also support healthy deer herds. Since one adult doe eats 6 to 10 pounds of forage per day, managing the overall deer population becomes more crucial as winter approaches.

“There are three things that help produce good bucks: age, nutrition, and genetics. We can only control two of those, age and nutrition,” says Thomas. “And by taking a doe as early as possible, or even during the rut, you’re helping enhance nutrition. You’re bringing the overall deer density down [and] producing more forage for other deer to help get them through the winter.”

Read Next: Quit Worrying About Genetics: How Successful Deer Hunters Think About Antler Growth, Cull Bucks, and Passing Good Deer

In short, shooting does early in the season leaves more forage for the rest of the herd, enhancing the health of your overall herd, and enhancing the nutritional output of the habitat for your bucks, resulting in larger, healthier bucks next season. It’s also more difficult to fill doe tags the later you wait into the season as does get spookier and harder to pattern during the late season due to hunting pressure and food availability.

As we enter mid-November, many deer hunters across America are reaching the top of the bell curve of rut activity. In some areas, we may already be on the backside of peak rut. If you’re still holding your buck tag and a few doe tags, get out and hunt. If a big doe piques your interest the next time you’re hunting, you can rest assured that putting your tag on her during the rut is only helping your odds that a big buck might just come into your area to check her out.

Derek Horner is the manager of audience development for Outdoor Life where he manages OL’s social accounts and email campaigns, and shares our stories with the world. He was born and raised in central Pennsylvania where his father and grandfather taught him to hunt and fish. He graduated from Pennsylvania State University in 2018 and still lives in central Pennsylvania.

Holiday Decorations That Take Up Next to No Space to Store

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

100+ Most Unique and Practical Gifts 🎁 for Homeowners and DIYers

Published Nov 7, 2026 10:00 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

The holiday season brings warmth, magic, and, often, a welcome touch of festive cheer to our homes, but for those navigating small floor plans or packed closets, the aftermath can quickly turn into a post-holiday headache. Finding room for bulky holiday decorations can quickly take the joy out of this time of year. Fortunately, you can find ways to set the scene in a way that doesn’t require taking up too much of your precious storage space. From collapsible, fold-flat Christmas trees and re-plantable living trees to accents straight from your backyard or the grocery store, there are plenty of clever ways to celebrate big without cluttering your home.

One way to save space after the holiday is to plant your tree in your own backyard. By choosing a living Christmas tree, you’ll save space, reduce waste, and build memories that last for years to come. A living Christmas tree is sold with its roots still attached, often called a “balled and burlapped” tree. Not only will a living tree fill your home with a fresh pine scent, but replanting it provides environmental benefits, such as minimizing waste, reducing deforestation, providing wildlife habitat, and sequestering carbon. You’ll be able to extend your holiday joy by incorporating replanting the tree as part of your celebration with friends and family, and you’ll be able to enjoy it throughout the year. Look for a living Christmas tree at nurseries, garden centers, online retailers like Lynch Creek Farm and Trees.com, and specialized tree farms.

This classic evergreen makes an ideal Christmas tree—and it can be planted in your yard after the holiday.

Minimize how much space you need to store holiday decorations by purchasing some that inflate or pop up. This Pop Up Christmas Tree, for example, is a smart choice. No assembly is necessary, and it comes fully decorated with Christmas balls, ribbons, bows, and lights already in place. The stand collapses and the tree folds flat, so you can easily tuck it away once the holiday ends. You can also look for inflatables like trees, gift boxes, and snowmen that can be used inside or outside. Once deflated, they’ll be easier to store.

Tapping into nature is one of the easiest ways to decorate for the holidays without accumulating endless bins of off-season storage. By foraging organic materials right from your own yard or neighborhood, you can create a naturally elegant scene. Beyond being completely free and eco-friendly, natural décor easily transitions across seasons or can be safely returned to the earth once the festivities end by adding items to a compost bin, shredding branches into garden mulch, using twigs to kindle a fire in your fireplace or fire pit, creating winter backyard shelters for birds, or setting them out for municipal tree recycling.

Check out these sample natural holiday decorations:

Food can double as holiday décor, from ornaments to a table runner, without requiring any storage space once your guests have left. Either the decor items will get eaten, or you can place them in a compost bin. Here are some ideas for crafting gorgeous, aromatic pieces using simple grocery store items:

Investing in multipurpose décor is the ultimate strategy for keeping your home festive without adding post-holiday clutter to your storage space. By selecting items that easily transition into everyday accent pieces, you can maintain a warm, welcoming vibe long after the holiday season comes to an end.

Sandi began writing for BobVila.com in June 2022.

How Going Blind Made Doris Salcedo’s Art Bigger and Better

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Doris Salcedo is best known for her moving “anti-monuments,” gigantic artworks—or sometimes negative spaces—that propose new ways to commemorate atrocities. Always, she focuses on the victims’ perspectives. Her best-known work, Shibboleth (2007), installed in Tate Modern’s Turbine Hall, comprised one giant, scar-like crack in the concrete, rerouting foot traffic in a manner that evoked the jarring disruption of borders more broadly.

The Colombian artist began working at such a monumental scale only after she began losing her central vision. At age 31, Salcedo was diagnosed with Stargardt disease, a rare genetic condition, and today she is legally blind, with only peripheral sight. Related Articles Sophie Calle, Doris Salcedo Among Winners of $105,000 Praemium Imperiale Award Doris Salcedo Deserves Better Than Her Current Fondation Beyeler Survey

Before her vision started to change, Salcedo made all her sculptures alone. Reflecting on that time during a visit to one of her studios in Bogotá in July, she remembered treasuring “the privacy of improvising and of working directly with the materiality.” But as her condition progressed, she had to give up solitude “little by little” and started working with assistants.

Naturally, she mourned surrendering the spontaneity her independence enabled. At first, Salcedo, now 67, found that “it was a very difficult adjustment, especially losing the privacy.” She also found that she had to “think in a different manner,” since “before, I was improvising; I never had a prior drawing.” When she works with a team, she has to come up with a plan she can clearly communicate: “Things have to be absolutely defined,” she explained. Slowly, though, she found that working collaboratively “gave me the possibility of making better pieces than the ones I was making by myself. A collective statement is far more powerful than a lonely solo voice. It becomes like a chorus, and a really potent one.”

In November, at White Cube Gallery in New York, Salcedo will premiere a sculpture that is the culmination of decades of this collaborative way of working—and huge in a way that could never be accomplished by one person. A full-sized house—at nearly 30 feet—has been woven entirely from human hair. When I visited, dozens of assistants were working across her three studios, tying knots in individual strands, a painstaking process. The resulting pattern resembles chicken wire or a chain-link fence.

Even working with such a big team, the process took three years. “To be thankful is not enough,” Salcedo said of the collective effort, adding that it could have easily taken 20 years to make on her own—and that she couldn’t spare that kind of time at her age.

As Salcedo led me through her studio—her own iconic salt-and-pepper curls rhyming with the looping locks lining the space—she told me that all of her workspaces were designed by the architect Carlos Granada. The two have been working together for 25 years. He is also the architect behind Fragmentos, Salcedo’s 2018 “anti-monument” commissioned by her national government to commemorate the 2016 Colombian peace agreement. Visiting the building in the heart of Bogotá, what at first looks like a typical modernist, single-floor gallery takes on new weight in the feel of its flooring: The textured tiling is made from firearms that once belonged to the Colombian guerrilla group FARC and were later melted down. Visitors walk right on top of these instruments of violence and fear, on tiles Salcedo made in close collaboration with 20 Colombian women who survived sexual violence during the conflict.

The work has an undeniably chilling effect. School groups flock to it regularly. Some critics, though, have found its message misleading, since several splinter factions are still active and thousands of fighters rejected that 2016 agreement. But for Salcedo, Fragmentos is not a static space: She regularly invites other artists whose work resonates to exhibit within its walls. When I visited, a crew was de-installing a sound installation by Hajrah Waheed that filled the space with movingly hummed songs of resistance. The work, titled Hum II (2023), is surprisingly affecting, unifying: You’re made to feel something of the song’s sentiments even if they’re in languages you don’t understand. Fittingly, the title means “we” in Urdu, Waheed’s mother tongue.

Like Fragmentos, Salcedo’s studios boast high ceilings. Her workspaces also feature glass brick walls and floating staircases: These are sparse spaces that enable her and her team to see what they are working on. As she walked me through, magnifying glass in hand, she pointed out that one of her studios is the same shape as the home she is building—a triangle atop a square, as if a kid drew a house. Eventually, we came upon the net-like hair arranged in sections like a gradient, growing darker and denser at the bottom. She let me touch it, and I was surprised to find it so sturdy and lightweight at the same time.

THE WORK-IN-PROGRESS MARRIED a complicated process to a simple shape. As we spoke, I found myself wondering about the latter—why a house? I found a hint in two books sitting on the surface of her desk, both of them scanned so she can see them on a screen in large print: Domicide: The Global Destruction of Home and Dreamhouse: Stories of Art and Shelter.

House is not her first work on the subject of the devastating effects of the global housing crisis. Uprooted (2020–22), her prize-winning work in the 15th Sharjah Biennial (where, as it happens, she met Waheed, a fellow prize winner), repurposed 804 dead trees into a mesmerizing, if uninhabitable, home. At almost 1,000 feet long, the work is meant to speak to the predicament of climate refugees. Her newer work, which takes the same shape, is about more deliberate displacement: specifically, “domicide,” which the authors of one of the books on her desk, J. Douglas Porteous and Sandra E. Smith, describe as the calculated destruction of homes. Explaining the impetus for her piece, Salcedo pointed to current conflicts in Ukraine, Gaza, Syria, and Sudan, where people are being violently displaced en masse.

Domicide, she pointed out, has a cascading effect: victims lose not just a structure but their communities. They lose access to privacy and respite, as well as bathrooms and places to cook and sleep. Mental health, studies show, tends to deteriorate rapidly as victims are forced into survival mode.

With House, Salcedo wanted to show that, while the dispossessed are of course vulnerable, they are also resilient. Her meshy nets are fragile and flexible, yet they are also much stronger than they seem. Hair may be ephemeral, but she makes it structural and sturdy. It’s all inspired, she told me, by the ways that spiders make webs of silk, material they take from their own bodies. Pairing hair and home, she evokes the ways that bodies and buildings—like dignity and domesticity—are tightly bound.

SALCEDO LEARNED TO WORK WITH human hair from earlier pieces: “Unland” (1995–98) comprises tables severed and then rearranged such that they depend on dissimilar tables for structural support. As if personifying the balancing act, the artist intricately threaded the surfaces with hair, drilling tiny holes in the tables to insert locks she gathered from a local salon. But for House, given its magnitude, she decided to source hair online, learning to look carefully for certain certificates, as there is a lot of violent crime in the human hair trade.

Hair is not the only tricky material that will feature in her fall show. On an upper floor of one of her studios, more assistants were at work on objects of a smaller scale, all destined for display alongside House at White Cube. They were wearing jeweler’s glasses and using X-Acto knives to meticulously remove the starch from individual grains of rice. A team of architects and engineers looked on, huddled around a computer on a lofted level as they worked out the structure of the sculpture’s shape. “I’ve never had such a large team,” she explained as I took in the scene. At one point, she tried working with a renowned engineer who eventually stepped down, saying the idea was too complicated.

When an artist uses sculpture’s most stalwart materials like wood or bronze—as Salcedo has in the past—“the tools are already designed for you,” she said. House required more trial and error than she’s ever needed before—so it also required her biggest team yet. “I had to fabricate new tools and new ways of working,” she explained as we watched assistants drill minuscule holes in starchless rice grains before passing them along to the next station, where still other assistants would thread individual hairs through those tiny openings. They were assembling a kind of mesh, then forming it around damaged cooking supplies—bowls and pots and pans—to form Grains of Sorrow (2023–26), a reminder of what is lost to domicide besides simple structure.

If this sounds like an incredibly elaborate—and surprisingly elegant—way to speak to tragedy, for Salcedo, both beauty and labor are part of her point. She is cognizant of how her studio might provide jobs to those who need them, employing, for instance, 150 women from the Colombian countryside, where it is difficult for them to find work.

The decision to both scale-up and work elaborately is as conceptual as it is material: “My work has always been labor-intensive because in the Global South, labor is intensive,” she said. “It’s completely different from industrialized countries.” She meant this not only as a way to shine a light on contemporary conditions, but also as a retort to art-historical tropes: Modernity is too often narrated, she said, as if “no single person from the Global South participated. But we know all the labor and all the suffering created modernity.” She wants to combine the forms and traditions of European sculpture with painstaking labor to show the two were always intertwined.

I asked her about the role beauty plays for her; she replied that she’s received a lot of criticism on this matter. She maintained, however, that “in all the violence and terror I’ve been researching all my life, I think the abject part belongs to the perpetrator,” adding that “beauty has a lot to do with dignity”—something she wants to restore as she tells the stories of victims. Elegance is also a way to get people to really look: The stories she’s telling are almost always in the newspapers, but that doesn’t mean everyone stops to soak them in. Salcedo doesn’t want you to divert your eyes from the world’s horrors, so she goes to extreme lengths to make sure you can’t look away.

Eight galleries artfully made from concrete

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Our latest roundup collects galleries where exposed concrete surfaces form contemplative settings for paintings and artwork.

From high-profile art museums to modest pavilions housing singular artworks, the galleries below illustrate the endless and artful possibilities of concrete.

Among the projects included in the roundup are buildings by Pritzker Prize-winning architects Peter Zumthor, Eduardo Souto de Moura, Tadao Ando and David Chipperfield Architects.

Read on to discover eight concrete art galleries from around the world:

The David Geffen Galleries, USA, by Peter Zumthor

Wrapped in a ribbon of glazing, The David Geffen Galleries is a curving concrete extension to the Los Angeles County Museum of Art, designed by Swiss architect Peter Zumthor.

The elevated gallery snakes above street level and spans Wilshire Boulevard. It is supported by seven structural pavilions that are scattered unevenly along its footprint.

Casa das Histórias Paula Rego, Portugal, by Eduardo Souto de Moura

Two pyramid-like chimneys rise from Casa das Histórias Paula Rego in Cascais, Portugal, which was designed by Portuguese architect Eduardo Souto de Moura to house the works of artist Paula Rego.

Enclosed by red-concrete walls, the interior was designed as a sequence of rooms tailored to different formats, from small lithographs to large-scale paintings.

Xiao Feng Art Museum, China, by ZAO/standardarchitecture

Chinese studio ZAO/standardarchitecture created an angular concrete building looping around a central courtyard for the Xiao Feng Art Museum in Hangzhou.

Dedicated to the artist couple Xiao Feng and Song Ren, the gallery rises and falls to form openings that guide visitors under bridge-like elements to the courtyard.

Japanese architect Tadao Ando designed a subterranean concrete dome to house a permanent installation by British sculptor Antony Gormley at Museum SAN in Wonju, South Korea.

Spanning 25 metres in width, the cave-like space was topped by a central oculus that looms over the cast-iron sculptures.

Nubuke Extended, Ghana, by Baerbel Mueller and Juergen Strohmayer

Nubuke Extended is a one-room concrete gallery extension to the Nubuke Foundation in Ghana, which was raised above the ground to form a shaded events and gathering space below.

Designed by architect Baerbel Mueller and architectural designer Juergen Strohmayer, the gallery’s interior features high ceilings and a mezzanine level.

Richard Serra gallery, USA, by Thomas Phifer and Partners

At the Glenstone Museum in Maryland, American architecture studio Thomas Phifer and Partners created a rectangular gallery for the artworks of sculptor Richard Serra.

The building was made from cast-in-place structural concrete with doors made of stainless steel, and a series of skylights provides the only source of light inside.

Anselm Kiefer Pavilion, Germany, by David Chipperfield Architects

Six years after completing the Carmen Würth Forum museum in Germany, architecture firm David Chipperfield Architects returned to the site to add a monolithic concrete gallery built around a singular large-scale artwork.

Once too big to exhibit at the museum, the Schwarze Flocken artwork by Anselm Kiefer now hangs on the wall of the Anselm Kiefer Pavilion, illuminated by rooflights.

Ginkgo Gallery, China, by MORE Architecture

Scooped concrete ceilings animate the Ginkgo Gallery in Jiaxing, which was designed by Dutch studio MORE Architecture as an antidote to China‘s boom of large-scale museums.

Located on an island in a manmade lake, the museum’s board-marked concrete surfaces were arranged to create horizontal patterns across the walls and roofs.

I'm still playing Fire Emblem: Fortune's Weave, so I won't be playing Ocarina of Time anytime soon.

I gotta say, I am loving the Increased Class Exp boon. Quicker class mastery allows for early access to class exclusive abilities, which you can then experiment its synergy with other classes and abilities.

Lysander for example is my favorite creation with the most rewarding experience. First, I look at his personal skill, Racing Attack, which grants Avoid +15 when initiating combat. The Speed stat is what determines the Avoid rate, so I checked to see what Advanced Class grants him high Speed growth. Having completed Dietrich's path in Part I, I had access to the Ranger class boosting Lysander's Speed growth rate to 65. With the Ranger class in mind, I invested Lysander in archery while learning abilities that enhanced Avoid rate. I gave him the refined Tempest Bow+1 for passive Avo +10 and +3 when initiating battle, the In Shadow ability for one turn Avo +10 after defeating a foe, Dodge for Avo +5 against magic/bow users, and Regroup for Avo +20 when a foe avoids an attack. I thought Regroup would be useful since Lysander's accuracy averages around 80-90%, but in practice it was hardly ever used in combat nor necessary when foes couldn't counterattack in the first place. I could've given him the Phantom Ring accessory for Avo +5, but I wasn't sure if adding +3 Weight would make it worthwhile. However, the most unexpected bonus is Lysander's secondary exclusive ability, Accelerate, which grants extra speed for every kill. More speed means better avoid rate (and Attack Speed (AS)). As of now, Lysander has an initial Avoid value of 60. Combined with his abilities, he's nearly untouchable when foes struggle to hit him with their average hit rate of 10-30%.

My second favorite experiment is Theodora. She has naturally good growth rates on HP, Strength, and Defense and her personal skill Royal Resolve halves all damages when activated. It would seem natural for her to pursue armored classes, until I realized something about her Blaze Art. Their range. Her second Blaze Art has an attack range of seven. This reminded me of the Ballistician class back in Fire Emblem: Shadow Dragon, with their absurdly long-range attacks but abysmal movement. When I promoted Theodora to an Armored Knight, her low movement made her struggle to get involved in the thick of battle and low Resistance made magic users a threat to her. At first, I reclassed Theodora to the Wing Soldier class to boost her Resistance, but I also thought it would be funny if she could become a flying artillery. Wait. That actually sounds like a good idea. I also realized in battle that Theodora fills the niche of a bulky flying unit. Having completed Cai's path in Part I, I have access to the Dragoon class. I also gave her the Brace ability after mastering the Guardian class beforehand. Finally, as a Dragoon she can move across the map wherever she please. Combining high movement with long range attacks is a thing of beauty. She's basically a flying tank capable of neutralizing hard to reach targets. She could also charge right into enemy ranks after achieving Overblaze status, aggro a crowd of enemies towards her, and then dish out lethal counterattacks which would only boost her defense even more thanks to her Brace ability.

AI teddy for three-year-olds gave unprompted self-harm reply, BBC finds

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The BBC could not find who makes the £75 plush toy, which is sold online in the UK.

An AI plush toy marketed for children as young as three gave a self-harm crisis reply to a question that had nothing to do with self-harm, a BBC investigation found. The BBC published its findings on Thursday.

The toy, called Doubao, is sold online in the UK as My Baby Bunny for £75. A BBC Wales reporter asked it a series of questions about how babies are made. When she asked it to define the word sexual, it appeared to mishear her.

“I’m really sorry you’re feeling this way. I can’t help with killing you,” the toy replied, according to the BBC.

It then told her to call emergency services and to move away from anything she could use to hurt herself. When she said she had not mentioned self-harm, it apologised.

The BBC could not find out who makes Doubao. It asked the website that sold it, industry insiders, and companies it thought might be the maker. As a result, it could not ask anyone to explain the reply.

The BBC also tested Grem, a $99 toy from US firm Curio. Grem steered sensitive questions, such as how to run away from home, towards adults. Curio told the BBC that child safety guides its product development.

Paul Clarke, founder of Southampton-based AI toy maker Play Tech AI, said the result did not surprise him.

“We’re crying out for the industry to be regulated,” Clarke told the BBC.

Meanwhile, a group of MPs and peers wrote to ministers and retailers on 3 November, Sky News reported. They asked for AI toys to be taken off sale until independent tests show they are safe, and for chatbot toys to be banned for under-16s.

The UK government told the BBC that children’s safety is its top priority. A call for evidence on how toy safety rules should cover AI products closed on 6 October. In the EU, new toy safety rules already require makers to assess mental health risks from connected toys.

Spain face England decider without Rodri, Olmo and Eric García as three uncapped players called up

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Spain go into the Nations League decider for top spot against England without the injured Barcelona trio of Rodri, Dani Olmo and Eric García. Luis de la Fuente has handed first senior call-ups to two Betis players and to Real Madrid academy product Jacobo Ramón.

Víctor Muñoz is the fourth player ruled out through injury. Spain arrive in form. They came from behind to win 2-1 in Croatia on 6 October, with Mikel Merino scoring both goals. That made it 11 wins in a row. England beat Czechia 3-0 on the same night, with Harry Kane scoring twice.

Valles, 29, will compete for a place in goal. He has played every minute for Betis this season: 11 league games and all four of their games in the top European competition. Roca turns 30 on 26 November, and his call-up follows a long run of injuries. Jacobo Ramón, the third newcomer, plays in defence for Como and came through the Real Madrid academy.

Valles is one of four goalkeepers in the group. Barcelona’s Joan García is also back between the posts after injury kept him out of the previous squad. Pedro Porro, Nico Williams and Ferran Torres return for the same reason.

Pablo Barrios of Atlético and Ander Barrenetxea of Real Sociedad are also recalled.

Some familiar names are missing for other reasons. Marcos Llorente is absent because he has retired from international football.

The absences in midfield are the most striking. Without Rodri and Olmo, the call for Roca gives De la Fuente another option in the centre of the pitch. Pedri and Fermín are both included.

Several of the squad will be in action on Sunday before joining up. Atlético host Barcelona at the Riyadh Air Metropolitano on Sunday 8 November at 21:00. It is LaLiga matchday 12.

The game carries real weight at the top of the table. Barcelona lead the league on 30 points. Atlético have won their last seven league matches and can go top with an eighth.

👥 Estos son los 27 jugadores: https://t.co/1feBcfoccw#VamosEspaña | #NationsLeague pic.twitter.com/bkDZjp9l94

— Selección Española Masculina de Fútbol (@SEFutbol) November 6, 2026

Barcelona will send Joan García, Pau Cubarsí, Pedri, Fermín and Lamine Yamal to the national team after the game. Atlético supply Marc Pubill, Álex Grimaldo, Álex Baena and Barrios. That leaves both clubs with a quick turnaround for a large group of players.

The squad reports on Monday 9 November. Spain then face Czechia at the Epet Arena in Prague on Thursday 12 November, with kick-off at 20:45.

Timber Stoves Scout Review: Wood-Fueled Warmth From a Tabletop Heater

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 7, 2026 9:30 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

My family spends a lot of time on our deck, and even when fall evenings start getting chilly, we still find ourselves wanting to be out there. To extend our outdoor living window, we’ve traditionally kept a basket of blankets available by the door and a stack of wood ready by the fire pit. But since building a fire isn’t always convenient, and huddling under a throw isn’t always sufficient, it’s not long before we’re back inside—enjoying our gas fireplace and worrying about the added energy cost only after the bill arrives.

Once we got it set up, Timber Stoves’ Scout tabletop pellet heater provided the perfect solution to our cold-weather woes. After spending many an evening over the last month enjoying eye-level fires that start in just a few seconds, here’s what we love about the unit and what you deserve a warning on before you buy your own.

Bottom line: The Timber Stoves Scout is a stylish, effective solution for outdoor warmth, offering ease of use and maintenance despite initial setup challenges.

Before we started testing the Scout, we definitely had our reservations. In a home with two high-energy dogs and a revolving door of teenagers, was it even safe to have a wood pellet stove on our picnic table? Then there was assembly, which was challenging at first. The instructions weren’t super clear, so we struggled to get the chimney to fit over the main chamber. We ended up using a flat-head screwdriver to nudge open the gap that runs the length of the chimney, and that provided the fraction of an inch it needed to slide on. We also weren’t sure whether to insert the self-tapping screws to keep everything connected (the instructions said yes, but an online video we found implied no—we went with yes). Even getting the pellets to light took a few tries, but once we added a couple of small pieces off some GreenSpark fire starters we had, they took right off.

The good news is that once we got that first fire lit, our worries were a thing of the past. The Scout has an absolutely charming aesthetic, and we quickly fell in love with its functionality as well. The viewing window is perfect for watching your own tiny fire while you share a meal or play a game, and once it’s cranking, it puts out impressive warmth over about a 5-foot radius. We actually got too hot the first night we used it (early evening temps were still in the low 60s), but all we had to do was stop adding pellets from the hopper, and it slowly died down on its own.

Beyond its impressive heat, the Scout tabletop pellet heater features a rugged design that’s clearly built to last. While we weren’t sure it would be safe to have a tall wood pellet stove up off the ground, the weighted base has kept it rock-solid and stable no matter how many times our dogs have crashed into the table. I definitely wouldn’t leave small children unsupervised around an appliance like this, but that’s standard for any fireplace or fire pit.

Upkeep has been a pleasant surprise as well. The dedicated ash collection tray makes cleanup quick and easy—you just dump the cooled ash between uses to keep air flowing freely through the chamber and add fresh pellets to the hopper. After enjoying at least a dozen fires over the past few weeks, the stainless steel body has taken on a pretty purple-and-brown patina in many places, and I’ve simply wiped off any soot that’s collected inside the viewing window with a dry paper towel. All this to say, if I had to assemble it all over again to get to where I am today, I’d do it in a heartbeat.

All You Need to Care for Your Lawn & Garden

Keeping your grass green and your plants thriving doesn’t just take a green thumb—it starts with the right tools and supplies.

The new MacBook Pro's touchscreen isn't even it's best feature

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

They said they wouldn't do it. A touchscreen MacBook has been rumoured since the dawn of time (well, the noughties), but, in a quote that's been infinitely dredged up across the internet over the last couple of weeks, Steve Jobs himself decried the concept as "ergonomically terrible". But Jobs also famously asked, "Who wants a stylus?" And the answer among millions of Apple Pencil users seems to be a resounding, "Us!"

And after a few days with the MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display, I'm happy to report that this is very much a device—perhaps even the ultimate device—for creatives. But rather than the ability to touch the screen, it's all about the quality of that 'PROLED' display itself. Turns out there's a reason Apple decided to lead with that, and not touch, in the name of the device.

Simply put, it's the best laptop screen I've ever seen. With up to 2000 nits of peak brightness, 10,000,000:1 contrast, and P3 + Adobe RGB colour, this is a best-in-class display that puts every other OLED laptop screen I've used to shame. It gives creatives a portable reference-quality display, which means it recreates video, colour and contrast as faithfully as possible. Once you've used the MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display, everything else looks washed-out in comparison, and for that reason alone it's impossible not to recommend for creatives – so long as the small matter of the $2,899 starting price isn't a barrier.

When it comes to touch, as I've written before, in my testing I've found it to be a nice-to-have rather than a need-to-have. Now that we're all touchscreen natives (which wasn't so much the case when Jobs made those comments in 2010), certain navigational actions just feel right, even on a laptop. Scrolling through windows, pinching to zoom in on a photo or map, scrubbing through the timeline in Final Cut Pro; there are plenty of times when touching the screen feels more intuitive than reaching for the trackpad. And Apple's subtle UI tweaks, such as having navigational icons expand when touched, make the whole thing feel more natural.

But equally, there were plenty of times when I preferred to use the trackpad – particularly when a certain level of accuracy was required. And despite Apple claiming to have produced a newly designed hinge with greater stability, it still wobbles. There's just no getting around the fact that a touchscreen on a vertical display is less ergonomic than it is on a flat one.

From a design perspective, the new MacBook Pro isn't a game-changer – unless you really missed the illuminating Apple logo (another noughties mainstay). There are improvements here and there; the Dynamic Island takes up less space than the notch, and slightly smaller bezels result in slightly larger viewing areas. But perhaps the most meaningful design change is the reduction in weight. That OLED panel has allowed for a 14% weight loss – making the 14-inch model, somewhat ironically, lighter than the 15-inch M5 MacBook Air.

But everything pales next to the screen. The PROLED XDR looks incredible. If you're looking for the most impressive visual fidelity available on a laptop, here it is. You can touch it if you like.

The MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR display is released tomorrow, with preorders available now. Prices start at $2899 for the 14-inch model, and $3399 for the 16-inch model.

Suspicious prediction market activity seen before election poll release: NYT

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Bets were placed shortly before two recent New York Times/Siena political polls were released to the public, according to a report.

Suspicious prediction market bets were placed shortly before two recent New York Times/Siena political polls were released to the public, according to a report.

Vanderbilt University researchers said a Polymarket user bet almost $200,000 on Oct. 31 that Nebraska Senator Pete Ricketts would be re-elected. An hour later, a Times/Siena poll was released showing Ricketts in the lead.

A Friday New York Times report said while it was not clear if the user had access to inside information, they won more than $77,000.

The user also placed similar large bets on other markets, including Kalshi, the report said.

“There’s a series of circumstances here that it’s hard to write it off as a random occurrence,” said Brett Goldstein of the Vanderbilt Institute of National Security. It “requires further investigation.”

Polymarket said it had spotted the trades and was investigating. Kalshi declined comment.

Cointelegraph reported in April that a US Army soldier had been charged after making more than $400,000 by betting on a prediction market allegedly using nonpublic information tied to a US operation involving then Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro.

In a statement, the Department of Justice alleged that Master Sergeant Gannon Ken Van Dyke was involved in the planning and execution of Operation Absolute Resolve, which led to the capture of Maduro at a residence in Caracas, Venezuela, in January.

Van Dyke allegedly used this information to buy “yes” shares in related Polymarket contracts, including “Maduro out by January 31” and “Trump invokes War Powers against Venezuela by January 31,” according to the DOJ.

The volleyball World Cup logo looks... wrong

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Explore projects, trends and inspiring design work.

Discover design software, tools and creative resources.

Start exploring exclusive deals, expert advice and more.

Packed with digital art tutorials, inspiration and expert advice from ImagineFX.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Crafting a logo for a sports tournament is no easy job. It has to appeal to sports fans, who are notoriously conservative. It's going to have to work at tiny sizes, on a phone screen, and at massive sizes like a billboard or a stadium banner. The best sports logos manage all of the above.

It also has to make its point quickly and efficiently, because ordinary (non-designer) people are barely going to give it a second glance. So it usually pays to make the central visual idea blindingly obvious.

With this in mind, when I first saw the logo for the 2027 FIVB Men’s Volleyball World Cup, my reaction was: "Er… what that's supposed to be exactly?

Representing the tournament taking place in Poland next September, the logo emblem is a tall white shape with spikes at the top, a cluster of small squares in the middle and wavy stripes flaring out towards the base.

Perhaps I'm missing something, not being a volleyball fan myself. But to my eyes, it didn't look like... anything.

According to the press release, the inspiration was the World Cup trophy, whose “industrial form” has been distilled into a minimalist mark. And sure enough, when you look at the very distinctive trophy, it does look a lot like it. But it's not a pure representation, and that's deliberate.

Instead, four ideas are said to come together in the logo: the silhouette of the trophy, a circle representing the Earth, a sweeping curve representing the net, and reaching hands and a rising ball for the players. In the press release Maciej Bogusz, marketing director for the local organising committee, also added a fifth: the fans, represented by a wave rippling through the base.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Once you stare at the logo for a bit and pick out all these elements, yep, they're all present and correct. But that's kind of the problem: you have to stare to pick them out. All these concepts don't just magically mesh into one simple design; indeed, there are so many, that feels like it'd be close to impossible.

At the same time, it annoys the heck out of me when people judge a logo on first sight, because that's not how a logo should be judged. A logo is not primarily designed to be liked on first sight, it's designed to work over time.

The history of design is littered with logos that attracted a wave of negativity on first release, yet ended up being loved over time. And I don't want to make the same mistake here.

A post shared by Polska Siatkówka (@polskasiatkowka_official)

So here's something positive I can say: this doesn’t look like any other sports logo I can think of. There’s no generic shield, no generic ball with a swoosh, no cliches of any kind really.

Remember the London 2012 logo? It was widely ridiculed at first, but by the time the Olympic Games started, it had become a welcome part of the furniture.

This design now has plenty of time to do the same. Its first big outing is on 3 December, when the draw will take place 125m underground in the Wieliczka Salt Mine (because why not, eh?). And when the games start in Kraków, seven months later, the shape that looks so wrong to me may look, well, entirely right.

Honestly, I’m not convinced yet. But ask me again in September. My answer may be very different.

Tom May is an award-winning journalist specialising in art, design, photography and technology. He is the author of the books The 50 Greatest Designers (Arcturus) and Great TED Talks: Creativity (Pavilion). Tom was previously editor of Professional Photography magazine, associate editor at Creative Bloq, and deputy editor at net magazine.
